Taylor Sheridan’s ‘Dutton Ranch’ Season 2 Has To Escape This ‘Yellowstone’ Trap

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dutton Ranch might bid farewell to the bittersweet Montana ranch, but trouble still awaits Beth Dutton (Kelly Reilly) and Rip Wheeler (Cole Hauser) in Rio Paloma, Texas. The couple has seen their fair share of extreme highs and lows in Yellowstone, and after its tragic finale, they've had enough violence to last a lifetime. In Taylor Sheridan’s spin-off, Beth, Rip, and their adopted son, Carter (Finn Little), pack their bags and head south in search of a fresh start.

Unfortunately, leaving Montana doesn't mean they can leave their family legacy behind. Their reputation is bound to follow them to Texas, bringing a new set of problems that Beth and Rip just can't seem to shake off. No matter how much they've changed or how far they get from the Yellowstone ranch, the skeletons in their closets always find a way to catch up with them.

Roughly six months after the Yellowstone finale, Beth, Rip, and Carter leave Montana for good after a wildfire strikes the property. Heading all the way south, the family settles on a 7,000-acre homestead near Rio Paloma in South Texas, determined to build their ranch from scratch. However, the local 10-Petal Ranch, led by its hardened matriarch Beulah Jackson (Annette Bening), doesn't take kindly to their arrival, sensing new competition in the area.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

But just like the Duttons, the Jackson family has problems of its own. Between an alcoholic son and a granddaughter whose resentment toward the ranch is driving a wedge between her and her father, Beulah is struggling to keep the 10-Petal legacy intact. And just when the Duttons begin settling into their new home, hoping to give Carter a safer future and finally find some much-needed peace themselves, things take a turn when Rip discovers a corpse on their property in the middle of the night.

Like everyone in Yellowstone, Beth and Rip are notoriously prone to aggression, whether they're dishing it out or on the receiving end. Beth has learned to mask her trauma by making herself untouchable. Anyone who messes with her, be it Jamie (Wes Bentley), Summer (Piper Perabo), or a random woman hitting on her husband at a bar, is bound to get a beating. The same goes for Rip. As the de facto enforcer of the Dutton Ranch, it's his job to protect the property at all costs. That means unleashing a raging bull into a bar or confronting a motorcycle gang with nothing but a branding iron.

Things are different in Dutton Ranch. Beth and Rip now have Carter to look after, and the kind of life they led in Montana could put their son's future at risk. Beth is particularly aware of this, refusing to let Carter follow in their footsteps as a rancher and pushing him to finish school instead. Although they're still knee-deep in the ranching business, their priorities have shifted. Rather than getting caught up in blind ambition that defined Yellowstone, Beth and Rip are more concerned with keeping their family afloat and building a home where Carter can have a better future. Local competition still threatens their livelihood, but they're no longer looking to seize everything in sight.

Unfortunately, trouble never stays away for long in Dutton Ranch. Ironically enough, Beth and Rip aren't even the ones starting it. Expanding their modest ranch while competing against a local cattle dynasty is already hard enough, but no amount of ranching experience can prepare them for the messes that keep landing on their doorstep. A dead body turns up on their property, and their cattle contract foot-and-mouth disease (FMD), forcing them to lose their entire herd overnight.

Kayce has his work cut out for him in this exclusive first look.

But the real danger starts when Beth and Rip get caught up in the 10-Petal Ranch drama. They initially step in to help the struggling Jackson family, only to stumble upon a much bigger operation involving cattle smuggling and fentanyl trafficking. That's a whole different level of criminal activity, even by Dutton standards. And if there's one sign that Dutton Ranch is heading back toward Yellowstone's bloody roots in Season 2, it's the Season 1 finale. After Beth and Rip expose the operation, cartel associates retaliate by kidnapping Carter. Beth and Rip wanted a fresh start in Texas, but with Carter taken, their turbulent past is catching up with them. Come Season 2, Dutton Ranch looks ready to go down the same bloody path as Yellowstone.

Seeking a fresh start beyond Yellowstone’s past, Beth and Rip confront relentless obstacles and a powerful competing ranch unwilling to yield control.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: IRMAA surcharge increases for 2027

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If one of the values for a 12-month period is not reported, then a reasonable, but not officially documented, approach is to use the average of the 11 reported values.
I don't think that would be a good approach at all. If an early value were missing, then that average would skew too high. If a late value were missing, then that average would skew too low. Off the top of my head, I think averaging the prior and following months to come up with a synthetic amount to use for the missing value would be a much more "fair" approximation. Using a geometric rather than arithmetic mean would be even better.

SharonAI: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Its secured-debt funding model means every GPU is backed by a customer contract, so the cash-flow test is whether those contracts convert to billing.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 21, 2026 at 11:14PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, SharonAI (SHAZ -18.06%) introduced an annualized run rate (ARR) revenue metric to track its commercial expansion as data center infrastructure comes online. Commercial execution now determines whether its contractual backing turns into actual cash flow. Three things management said on the call stand out.

The company's annualized run rate revenue, or projected yearly revenue based on active billing, will surge as 30 megawatts of data center power capacity enter active service. That operational activation converts initial infrastructure installations into cash-generating deployments under long-term agreements, with billing expected in January of 2027.

Rather than tapping high-cost corporate borrowing, management finances graphics processing unit (GPU) hardware using special purpose vehicles (SPVs), or isolated subsidiary companies created for specific infrastructure projects. These debt facilities lock in funding directly against customer agreements where clients must pay regardless of usage.

Customer pricing on multiyear agreements has increased as specialized compute capacity grows scarce across global markets. High contribution margins, or the proportion of revenue remaining after direct operating costs, allow project returns to pay back initial equipment costs well inside contract terms.

Customers are scrambling for immediate power and compute access, and management is not hiding its satisfaction about its secured data center footprint. The company holds pre-connected power infrastructure in Australia and an anchor partnership with NVIDIA, giving it clear advantages over rivals stuck in utility grid delay queues. Management keeps a close watch on cluster deployment timelines and debt execution.

Converting a total contract value of $9.6 billion into active billing remains the ultimate test for the business as it ramps hardware deployments into 2027.

Netflix Falls Below $60. Bill Ackman Explains Why That’s an Incredible Investment Opportunity.

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A lower share price in the short term could be great in the long run.

By Adam Levy – Nov 21, 2026 at 11:00PM EST

Netflix's (NFLX -2.96%) share price has been cut in half from the all-time high it reached last summer. The stock now trades below $60 per share, as of this writing.

While there are reasons for the huge drop in Netflix, including slowing revenue growth and concerns about engagement, the drop prompted billionaire investor Bill Ackman to add the stock back to Pershing Square's (PS -8.77%) portfolio earlier this year. The stock has continued to drop, though, but Ackman doesn't see that as a concern. In fact, he sees it as an incredible opportunity for investors.

To be sure, Netflix's revenue growth has slowed considerably this year. Revenue grew between 15.9% and 17.6% in the four quarters through the first quarter of this year. That rate slowed to 13.4% in the second quarter and 11.8% in the third quarter. Management forecasts just 11.5% growth in the fourth quarter.

Management wasn't afraid to touch on the reasons for the slowdown. Co-CEO Ted Sarandos said the U.S. is "just not growing as fast as it could or as fast as we'd like," during the company's third-quarter earnings call. The advertising business has also been weaker than expected. "We are currently a bit behind our aggressive target for the year," Co-CEO Greg Peters said on the call. And that stems from disappointing engagement. "We want to grow view hours faster. We're not satisfied. We're working on it," CFO Spencer Neumann said.

But investors may be overlooking a key detail about Netflix's business that hasn't changed even as revenue growth slows. Management is highly effective at dialing in content spend and reaching its target operating margin. Operating margin is on track to reach 31.5% this year, up from 29.5% last year.

What's more, Netflix's free cash flow conversion is very high and growing. It generated $9.5 billion of free cash flow through the first three quarters of the year. Netflix uses that cash to buy back shares, which supports significant improvements in its earnings per share.

That's where Ackman believes the current share price makes Netflix stock a compelling buy. "We'll make more money over time if the stock is cheaper in the short- to intermediate-term because they're going to cancel a lot more shares as a result," he said in a recent interview.

Netflix repurchased $4.8 billion of its stock in the third quarter, and it still has $22.3 billion in authorization capacity. At the company's current share price, the remaining authorization represents around 9% of the current share count.

Netflix could complete the remaining authorization over the next two years. Management expects to generate about $2.5 billion in free cash flow in the fourth quarter, and that number should steadily climb next year. The company should be able to maintain its double-digit revenue growth rate for the foreseeable future with improvements in advertising, pricing, and maintaining a low churn rate while operating margin continues to expand. That should result in higher free cash flow conversion, meaning free cash flow grows even faster than earnings.

Ackman and his team project earnings-per-share growth of 19% per year over the next three to five years, driven by Netflix's share repurchase program. The market is pricing Netflix shares at just 22 times its free cash flow, and considering that cash is going right back to shareholders through buybacks, it seems like a fantastic opportunity for investors.

58 Years Later, Captain Kirk's Best Star Trek Quote Is the Greatest Line in Sci-Fi History

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This Captain Kirk speech says more about what Star Trek stands for than any other line in the original series.

Some lines of dialogue become so famous they stop belonging to the movies and TV shows that produced them. “May the Force be with you.” “I’ll be back.” “You're gonna need a bigger boat.” You don't need to know the context. The words have long since transcended their original context and permeated the pop culture zeitgeist.

Star Trek: The Original Series has plenty of those. Spock’s wish that everyone to “Live long and prosper” is arguably the franchise's signature phrase. He also turned the simple word “fascinating” into a catchphrase simply by raising one eyebrow before saying it. Dr. McCoy (DeForest Kelley) spent three seasons reminding everyone, “I'm a doctor, not a…” and, at least in our collective memory declaring, “He's dead, Jim” while standing over the latest redshirt who wandered a little too far from the landing party.

But if I could only pick one line to represent everything Star Trek stands for, it would be four simple words spoken by Captain James T. Kirk (William Shatner) halfway through “Return to Tomorrow," the 20th episode of season 2:

On the surface, it doesn't seem like an obvious choice. It's not printed on T-shirts nearly as often as “live long and prosper.” It isn't endlessly quoted by people who have never watched an episode of Star Trek. And unlike “beam me up, Scotty,” it never became part of the broader pop culture vocabulary. But that's exactly why it's the best quote. It doesn't just capture Kirk. It captures the entire philosophy of Star Trek.

First broadcast on Feb. 9, 1968, the episode “Return to Tomorrow” finds the Enterprise answering a mysterious distress call from a dead planet. There, Kirk and his crew discover Sargon, one of three survivors of an ancient civilization that has existed as beings of pure energy for half a million years. Sargon asks Kirk, Spock (Leonard Nimoy), and Dr. Ann Mulhall (Diana Muldaur) to temporarily surrender their bodies so he, his wife Thalassa, and the ambitious Henoch can build android bodies and begin life again. Dr. McCoy believes the aliens are manipulating them. Kirk believes the chance to learn from a civilization hundreds of thousands of years more advanced than humanity is worth the danger.

The episode also marks the first of three Star Trek appearances by Diana Muldaur. She plays Dr. Ann Mulhall here before returning as Miranda Jones in the third season episode “Is There in Truth No Beauty?” Years later, she'd become a series regular on Star Trek: The Next Generation as Dr. Katherine Pulaski. “Return to Tomorrow” is also a fun piece of Trek trivia because it's one of the times Spock describes Sargon as “pure energy,” a phrase longtime fans immediately recognize.

Faced with allowing unimaginably powerful aliens to occupy the bodies of his crew, Kirk doesn't issue an order. He makes an argument and reminds his crew that every leap forward in human history required someone willing to embrace uncertainty.

"They used to say if man could fly, he'd have wings," Kirk says. "But he did fly. He discovered he had to." He likens Sargon's offer to humanity's own journey from the Moon to Mars and eventually the stars, arguing that the potential for knowledge outweighs the danger. Only then does he arrive at the four words that define both the captain and the franchise. Those four words explain Kirk better than decades of pop-culture impressions ever could.

For years, pop culture has reduced Kirk to an oversimplified caricature: the swaggering captain who punches first, flirts with every alien woman he meets, and somehow loses his shirt during every other away mission. The real Kirk is far more thoughtful than that. He takes risks, certainly, but not because he enjoys gambling. He takes them because he believes some opportunities are too important to ignore.

There's a world of difference between being reckless and accepting risk in pursuit of discovery. Kirk isn't arguing that danger is exciting. He's arguing that progress is impossible without it.

More importantly, he's explaining what separates Starfleet from almost every other organization. Plenty of fictional explorers travel because they're ordered to. Others do it for conquest, profit, or survival. Starfleet goes because knowledge itself is worth pursuing.

That's why I think this quote is even more meaningful than the famous opening narration about boldly going where no one has gone before. The opening tells us what the Enterprise does. This one explains why.

The timing of the episode makes the speech even more remarkable. “Return to Tomorrow” aired in early 1968, roughly 17 months before Apollo 11 landed on the Moon. America was still trying to prove humans could successfully reach another world, and Star Trek was already imagining the next step beyond that. Kirk's speech places the Enterprise in the same lineage as every explorer who came before it, suggesting humanity's future won't be built by playing it safe.

That idea has become the beating heart of Star Trek. Optimism, in the Star Trek universe, has never meant believing everything will work out. It means believing the unknown is worth facing anyway.

There are more famous quotes in The Original Series. There are funnier ones. There are certainly more quotable ones. But no other line distills what Star Trek has always been trying to say better than four words Kirk spoke nearly 60 years ago: “Risk is our business.”

Victor Wembanyama outscores Pelicans starters in first half, finishes with second 50-point game of season in Spurs win

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Victor Wembanyama outscored the New Orleans Pelicans' starters in the first half on Saturday night at home. He had 32 points. They had 26.

By game's end, Wembanyama had authored his second 50-point performance of the season, this time in a 114-91 victory over the Pelicans (5-13) at Frost Bank Center.

The 7-foot-4 superstar went for 52 points in a Nov. 4 win over the Dallas Mavericks. Both of those outings have coincided with San Antonio (14-3) going 14-1 following an 0-2 start.

Wembanyama, still only 22, has already tied Spurs legend David Robinson for the second-most 50-plus-point games in franchise history with three. George Gervin, a Basketball Hall of Famer like Robinson, leads the way with five of those games.

Wembanyama's got a long runway to surpass both Robinson and Gervin in that department. A third 50-piece this season would be historic in itself.

He's the third player since 1973-74 to log at least 50 points, 15 rebounds and 5 blocks in a single game. Wembanyama clocked out with 50 points on 20-of-29 shooting, 19 rebounds, 5 blocks and 4 steals.

The Spurs pulled away in the second quarter. San Antonio also got 19 points from guard Stephon Castle. Another backcourt contributor, De'Aaron Fox, piled up 10 assists.

The Pelicans are 1-9 in their last 10 games. They've come undone after beginning the 2026-27 campaign with a 4-4 record. A bright spot for New Orleans is the continued growth of 20-year-old guard Jeremiah Fears, who led the team with 29 points in the loss.

As for Wembanyama, he's once again an NBA MVP candidate. It's looking like he'll face stiff competition for that award this season as well.

Los Angeles Lakers phenom Luka Dončić has a pair of 50-point games under his belt, too, and he's leading the NBA in scoring with 37.2 points per contest.

“Race realism” isn’t realistic—and it isn’t good science

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Modern genetics does not support claims of innate racial differences in intelligence.

At this year’s World Economic Forum meeting in Davos, US President Donald Trump stated that people from Somalia have low IQs. “I always say these are low-IQ people,” he said in his speech. His son, Donald Trump Jr, made a similar statement about Haitian people in 2024 (Trump’s government has since ended the protected status of both Somali and Haitian migrants in the US).

These statements and their consequences are symptoms of a growing trend in Western politics: the re-emergence of racial views of human nature that have been largely in hiding for the past 70 years. Narratives about the innate intellectual inferiority of black or brown people compared with white people—which can still be found in academic journals today—are fuelling far-right propaganda in some of the world’s largest democracies. Such claims underpin misinformation strategies on social media and bolster political movements.

These narratives are sometimes euphemistically referred to as “race science” or more recently “race realism.” But, as we argue, they are not actually scientific. One member of this community, Nathan Cofnas, recently attracted public attention by accusing the late Jason Arday of plagiarism.

What got less attention was that Cofnas has links to a network of people who make supposedly scientific claims about the innate intelligence, criminality, or sexuality of different racial groups. Some within this network are affiliated with universities, giving their work the mask of legitimacy.

This group produces work that has the appearance of science, but which is methodologically deeply flawed and fails to engage with rigorous scientific research. Yet they regularly succeed in publishing this work in (a handful of) mainstream academic journals.

How do they manage to do this, and what are the consequences?

Contemporary race scientists position themselves as persecuted but legitimate scientists who are prevented from pursuing their work for ideological reasons. They sometimes refer to what they defend as hereditarianism. This is the idea that genetic differences play a key causal role in determining differences in human behavior and intelligence between human groups—such as races—over purely environmental differences.

Yet scientific evidence has shown repeatedly and from many points of view that hereditarian thinking is both flawed and wrong. Race scientists make extremely simplistic claims such as: “Black people have innately lower intelligence than white people, because selection pressures resulted in the evolution of higher intelligence in white people.” No part of this claim can be scientifically supported; not from the point of view of genetics, evolutionary biology, or psychology.

In the 1970s, the influential evolutionary biologist Richard Lewontin famously showed that there is more genetic variation within human groups than between them. Socially defined racial groups just aren’t that genetically different from one another. There is genetic variation—though we’re actually a relatively genetically similar species compared to closely related primates. But this variation doesn’t fall neatly into clusters that correlate with how we typically categorize races.

Skin tone and color do vary between geographical locations. So do some other physiological features like hair texture, or the ability to process oxygen or milk products. But these differences are associated with characteristics of the environment rather than with racial groups. Some genetic variants associated with white skin color, for example, may have originated in Africa. Similarly, the ability to digest milk is seen in some populations in Europe, Africa, and Asia, rather than being an exclusively “Aryan” characteristic, as far right groups sometimes claim.

We now know considerably more about the human genome than Lewontin did in the 1970s, but his conclusions are supported today. Knowing someone’s race just doesn’t tell us a great deal about their genetics.

Recent scientific advances allowing us to examine ancient DNA from archaeological sites help explain why genetic differences do not fall neatly along racial lines. This research paints a picture of constant mixing between human groups, as waves of migration washed to and fro across the globe. It ultimately makes any belief in the genetic “purity” of European or African populations meaningless.

While human genetics is fascinatingly complex, race scientists ignore this complexity in favor of a far-too-simplistic view of humanity. And this view is not supported by the work of scientists who have used rigorous scientific methods to further our understanding of our species.

When race scientists say that black people have lower intelligence than white people, they typically base that on claims of differences in IQ test results. But the data underlying these claims is often shaky, to say the least.

Hereditarians have relied heavily on a database that claims to provide average IQs for countries worldwide: the “national IQ” database. Researchers have repeatedly demonstrated that this database is deeply methodologically flawed. It isn’t possible to measure intelligence in the same way in very different cultures, with different exposure to formal education. That’s because we know that education is one of several other factors linked to differences in IQ.

The methods used to put the database together did not come even close to meeting typical standards of scientific rigor. There’s even analysis suggesting that low IQ samples were deliberately chosen to represent sub-Saharan Africa, to make the IQ for black populations artificially low.

Meanwhile, the Flynn effect, showing that IQ has risen considerably during the 20th and 21st centuries, clearly demonstrates that IQ scores are heavily determined by the environment we’re in. Such a rapid rise over a short period of time must have environmental, not genetic, causes.

What’s more, intelligence is a contested topic—something that is hard to define and measure. IQ tests only measure specific cognitive abilities, ignoring things like social intelligence or creativity, for example. In fact, social intelligence features heavily in mainstream research on the evolution of human intelligence.

As one of us recently outlined in a peer-reviewed paper, race scientists are largely silent on the social brain hypothesis—the view that humans evolved remarkable intellectual abilities to manage social issues. In every group, there is strong social competition around resources, mating, and status. These are the things humans spend the most time navigating—the complexities of our rich social lives.

Not one of the prominent mainstream views on the evolution of human intelligence concludes there are innate cognitive differences between racial groups. Factors that have been put forward range from our cooperative child rearing to our heavy reliance on culture and learning. These all involve our social relationships, and all have received equal lack of attention from race scientists.

Mainstream opinion in the evolutionary behavioral sciences is that cognitively, all human groups are the same, though of course individuals within groups differ. Our amazing behavioral diversity stems from our species’ ability to adapt flexibly to our environment. This is especially because of our reliance on culture and social learning—not because we have evolved different psychologies to suit different environments.

Rather than drawing on this mainstream research, race scientists use simplistic “just-so” stories to explain how race differences in traits such as intelligence supposedly evolved. Their “cold winters theory,” for example, claims that the challenges of cold winters in Europe and east Asia created evolutionary selection pressures for greater intelligence after human groups migrated out of Africa. In doing so, they make unsupportable assumptions about environmental differences between Africa and the rest of the world. They also fail to acknowledge half a century of research on the evolution of human cognitive ability.

Yes, we needed to learn new food gathering practices and methods for dealing with environmental challenges as we migrated across the world. But the same was true as humans migrated across the large and ecologically diverse continent of Africa. Race scientists provide no evidence for their arguments. They don’t rely on archaeological evidence, nor do they use typical methods from evolutionary biology to understand evolutionary processes. Using the methods of race scientists, it would be equally easy to make the argument that challenges posed by hot summers drove the evolution of higher intelligence, and equally without scientific backing.

No matter which way you look at these issues—from the view of genetics, anthropology, evolutionary biology, or psychology—hereditarianism does not hold up.

To understand the potential consequences of these views, it is important to see them in historical context. Early in the 20th century, race science was mainstream. Overly simplistic and biologically deterministic views about the origins of differences between human groups—not just race but class—were widespread (if not universal) and important to the eugenics movement.

If disadvantaged groups owe their disadvantage to some fault within them, and not to the structural conditions they experience, then some argue society is not responsible for helping those people. Such arguments led to eugenic sterilization laws that aimed to “improve” society by preventing—without their consent—the reproduction of those considered likely to pass on their innate “defects” to future children. And it was ultimately behind the Nazis’ “solution” of trying to eliminate “undesirables” entirely.

In the decades after the Second World War, the horrors of the Nazi regime led to a decline in the popularity of eugenics in mainstream research and a rejection of race science. But the belief that science supported eugenic claims was already collapsing, even as the Nazis were trying to implement a race-based society. As we learned more about human biology and genetics during the 20th century, it became obvious that human genetic variation was too complex to be mapped into socially recognized racial (or class) categories. Advances in science meant race science dropped out of mainstream thinking.

Yet those committed to a belief in a biological hierarchy of races, regardless of what the science said, wanted venues in which they could continue their discussions. In the 1960s, the pseudo-scientific journal Mankind Quarterly became their venue. Mankind Quarterly was funded by the Pioneer Fund, a nonprofit foundation set up in the 1930s by a US textile magnate who chose to use his fortune to fund research on race science and fight desegregation in the US.

The Pioneer Fund continued to fund race science into the 21st century—including supporting Mankind Quarterly—when it was taken over by the current network of race scientists. They channeled its remaining funds into a new company, the Human Diversity Foundation, subsequently rebranded as Polygenic Scores LLC.

Given all this, how is race science still being published in mainstream academic journals? To be clear, race science does not typically get published in genetics or evolutionary biology journals. Most academic journals operate peer review, meaning papers only get published after evaluation by experts in the field. The overall absence of race science in genetics or biology journals is a clear signal that it does not meet contemporary standards of scientific rigor in genetics or biology.

Instead, race scientists continue to publish in para-scientific journals. But race science also makes it into a growing number of mainstream social science or humanities journals. Psychologist Andrew Winston, an expert in the history of race science in psychology, has described it as a “community project… with its own networks, mentorships, journals, funding, conferences, heroes, origin myths, martyrs, defenders, and historiography.”

Behind the network of writers producing race science sits a community of editors, peer reviewers, and others in gatekeeping roles, which too often allows race science to be published in mainstream journals. This is particularly in the subfields of psychology interested in intelligence and individual differences, and evolutionary psychology.

Editors of Intelligence, the mainstream journal that has probably published the largest quantity of race science, have occasionally published editorials defending their publication of race science in the name of ensuring robust academic debate even on controversial topics.

There have been sanctions. Philippe Rushton, one of an earlier generation of race scientists, faced sanctions from his university in Canada but kept his job apparently owing to the intervention of Edward O. Wilson, arguably the most famous biologist toward the end of the 20th century.

Cofnas created justified controversy over his call for a “hereditarian revolution” after which it will be acknowledged that black people are innately intellectually disadvantaged compared to white people. But he kept his postdoctoral fellowship at Cambridge University (though his affiliation with a Cambridge college was ended). He has also kept his postdoctoral position at Ghent University after using the opportunity provided by excessive news coverage of Arday to publicize his hereditarian views (though some sanctions have been imposed, such as restrictions on teaching). Cofnas has defended his views by saying he’s been misunderstood and is entitled to free inquiry.

Moreover, when University College London discovered that the race science community had been holding conferences on its premises (the “London Conferences on Intelligence”), without their knowledge of the subject of the conferences, they acted to prevent further such conferences being held. The publisher of the journal Intelligence, Elsevier, has recently appointed new editors, who have stated their commitment to publishing both rigorous and socially responsible science. Race scientists are increasingly publishing in evolutionary psychology journals rather than Intelligence.

However, the London Conferences on Intelligence continue, though not at UCL. Race scientists also have a media arm, Aporia magazine, which promotes race science using much more overt language and with more obvious policy focus than publications in academic journals. One Aporia article, for example, argued for eugenics as a solution to Africa’s lack of economic development, noting in a post that some may find it “distasteful.” It claimed—relying on race science’s unscientific arguments—that artificial selection for higher intelligence is needed to improve the continent’s innately unintelligent populace.

Race science may not have the respect of the scientific community, but it does have funding and influential backers. A report by Hope not Hate revealed that the network of race scientists received significant funding from the US businessman Andrew Conru, who withdrew his funding after the report was released.

The US Ambassador to Belgium even intervened when Ghent University set up an investigation into how Cofnas was hired.

Race scientists are far from the victims they claim to be. Race scientists in this and previous generations have worked hard to ensure their messages are heard beyond academia. And in this generation, they are successfully using social media to spread their beliefs, especially Elon Musk’s X.

But what about academic freedom? Isn’t it good for science to discuss any hypothesis to ensure free exchange of ideas? Academic debate is productive only if it involves methodologically rigorous research. Science will not progress if one side of any debate consistently uses poor-quality, methodologically flawed work. Nor is it necessary to continually debate topics that have long since been settled.

We believe it is unequivocal that race science is fundamentally pseudoscience. In our view, it’s critical to call attention to not only the scientific flaws underlying this body of work but also the real target it’s aimed at: political actors and everyday readers who ultimately decide the direction that science, and society, goes in.

Jonathan R. Goodman is Assistant Research Professor, Psychiatry, University of Cambridge, and Rebecca Sear is Director of the Centre for Culture and Evolution, Brunel University of London.

NBC to Air Princess of Wales Cancer Charity Documentary

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The special follows the princess as she completes a challenge to climb the highest peaks in England, Scotland and Wales.

NBC will air a documentary special following the Princess of Wales as she completes a charity challenge to raise money for cancer patients.

Heal: A Princess of Wales Special is set to air at 8 p.m. ET/PT Nov. 30 on NBC, a day after it premieres on the BBC in the U.K. The special will also at 11:30 p.m. Nov. 30 on Bravo and stream on Peacock starting Dec. 1.

Heal will explore “life beyond diagnosis and the many ways people find comfort, strength and hope in the face of serious illness,” a description reads. Cameras followed Catherine as she completed the Three Peaks Challenge — climbing the tallest mountains in England, Scotland and Wales in the course of 24 hours — in June in support of the Royal Marsden Cancer Charity. It will also feature the stories of other people facing cancer (the princess was diagnosed with a form of cancer in 2024 but was in remission as of 2025).

“[Cancer] changes your body, it changes you physically,” Catherine says in a trailer for the special (watch it below). “But there’s a much, much deeper part to what a cancer diagnosis shifts in you.”

All money from distribution of the special (it will also air in Australia on Dec. 1 and Canada on Dec. 7) will go to the Royal Marsden Cancer Charity to help people living with and after a cancer diagnosis. Detail Films produced the special, with Benji Jackson directing.

Opinion: When should medical school start?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Torie Bosch is the First Opinion editor at STAT.

First Opinion is STAT’s platform for interesting, illuminating, and provocative articles about the life sciences writ large, written by biotech insiders, health care workers, researchers, and others.

To encourage robust, good-faith discussion about issues raised in First Opinion essays, STAT publishes selected Letters to the Editor received in response to them. You can submit a Letter to the Editor here, or find the submission form at the end of any First Opinion essay.

The authors’ proposal hinges on the premise that learning the foundational sciences and learning to become a physician can be separated without compromising either. My first year of medical school was peppered with clinical exposure and taught me otherwise. I floundered with a blood pressure cuff during a wellness visit, fumbled for words as I interviewed a man waiting for a heart transplant, and listened to a patient recount her immense sorrow after multiple miscarriages. These encounters remain among my most formative.

I agree with the authors that students are often preoccupied with medical licensure exams, and that early clinical exposure might not cure this. In my experience, however, it is the strongest counterweight. After meeting those patients, I was learning for more than an exam. The proposed model would abandon that counterweight. Students would still encounter patients eventually, but we would have forfeited valuable time. Becoming a compassionate, humanistic physician is slow work, and it deserves all the time we can offer. A flashcard can teach a student what causes a miscarriage. It takes a patient to teach what it means to grieve one. And that lesson should not wait until after Step 1.

— Surya Pulukuri, Harvard Medical School student

Markovich is correct: The U.S. health care “non-system” is broken. There are many culprits, including hospitals, whose prices are set more by market dominance than by quality. He should also look at the administrative complexity driven largely by our myriad of private insurance companies. Some 25% of U.S. “health care” spending goes to administrative costs. The insurers need to agree on a single set of billing codes and procedures to cut doctors’ and hospitals’ cost of billing. They need to sit down with doctors and come up with a uniform set of procedures needing pre-authorization and a single set of criteria. They need to agree on a small number of policy options that are understandable to the public. Finger-pointing is easy, but should start at home.

— Edward Hoffer, M.D., Harvard Medical School

A chatbot can handle every conversation responsibly and still, over time, take the place of someone a person used to confide in. In their First Opinion essay, Qian Yang, Ned Cooper, and Ibrahim Emara show how easily a gradual loss of human connection can escape safeguards designed for individual conversations. They make a persuasive case for giving independent researchers access to platform data and developing tools to distinguish the effects of chatbot use from other causes of isolation. Those tools should ask whether people could reach someone they trusted when they needed help.

That question is especially worth asking adolescents, who may have fewer ways to seek help privately. Imagine two teenagers who each spend an hour every night talking to a chatbot. One used to call a friend during difficult times, but he called less often as talking to the chatbot became a habit. The other still talks to friends every day. When she wanted to discuss something she feared would get back to her parents, she asked to see a school counselor. While she waited for an appointment, she took the problem to a chatbot. Their chat logs might look very similar. The authors suggest measuring school counselor time as one way to estimate what isolation costs schools. That measure captures time counselors spend helping students. Hours, days, or weeks spent waiting leave no trace.

Researchers could ask teens to think about the last time something was troubling them. Did they talk to someone about it, try to reach someone, or decide against reaching out? If they tried, did they get to talk to someone? Did they talk to a chatbot about it?

Asking these questions regularly could help researchers distinguish a teen who reached out to people less often as their chatbot usage grew from one who turned to a chatbot only after trying to get help. The distinction matters for policymaking. When chatbot use displaces human contact, usage limits may help. When it fills a gap in support, the priority should be making help easier to reach. A usage limit might prompt one teenager to call a friend. Another may still be waiting for someone to call back.

— Brian J. Carter, J.D., Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine

In her essay “How bad is healthy supposed to feel?” Kirsten Balin properly notes that medicine has a regrettable history of dismissing symptoms, particularly in women. However, she also says that health professionals tend to give an illness label to the aches, pains, and anxieties of everyday living when the symptoms have no underlying biological explanation. As examples, she lists chronic fatigue syndrome and autonomic dysfunction. In fact, there are tens of thousands of peer-reviewed publications in top scientific journals that document the biological abnormalities in these two illnesses. The same is true with several other similar illnesses such as fibromyalgia and long Covid. It now is clear that they all are “real” illnesses.

— Anthony L. Komaroff, M.D., Harvard Medical School

The authors of the First Opinion commentary on pediatric emergencies from Oct. 27 identify a growing but hidden problem: stark gaps in delivery of pediatric emergency care across the U.S. The solutions they propose — payment reform, expanding regional care coordination hubs, and incentivizing large pediatric medical centers to support smaller hospitals — all merit serious consideration.

However, such approaches risk overlooking existing strengths that we can build upon now to support emergency departments and prehospital agencies aiming to develop their own internal capacity. Pediatric readiness refers to the infrastructure and resources EDs and prehospital agencies need to provide high-quality care for children. In our roles as researchers and physicians, we collaborate in community-engaged research to identify strategies to support EDs and EMS agencies in their readiness.

This is vital: EDs with high pediatric readiness have up to 76% lower mortality for critically ill children. It would cost approximately $200 million to make all EDs nationwide pediatric ready, a tiny fraction of annual U.S. health care expenditures. Many EDs lack designated pediatric champions, knowns as pediatric emergency care coordinators (PECCs), whose presence has been shown to be closely associated with readiness. As the authors rightly point out, larger pediatric medical centers can support smaller hospitals and PECCs.

That’s why we collaborate with local, national, and international partners in readiness improvement strategies such as telehealth support to smaller hospitals, simulation-based systems-testing and medical education, developing PECC networking and mentorship models, and more. This research stems from our experiences in rural, indigenous, and urban hospital systems. This has helped us understand the importance of foregrounding community members’ concerns and supporting their local expertise and leadership in our research efforts.

The authors grounded their commentary in two stories of what pediatricians are seeing nationwide. We agree that stories from families and local providers should drive our research. For us, that means collecting stories about what pediatric emergency readiness should look like, collaborating with community hospitals and families to identify research questions and select appropriate methodologies, and finding sustainable dissemination and implementation strategies.

EDs can start their own readiness journey by participating in the National Pediatric Readiness Project’s national assessment to identify current strengths and opportunities for improvement. As we work toward a future where any child can receive high-quality emergency care when and where they need it, we recommend starting by identifying and building upon strengths already in place.

— Elizabeth Sanseau, M.D., Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia (CHOP) PolicyLab, CHOP Indigenous Health Unit, and the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine; and Jeremy Jones, M.D., CHOP PolicyLab

As a pediatric emergency physician and clinical ethicist, I have experienced and witnessed aggression at work many times. The authors’ concern comes from a good place, but it misses the realities of the clinical setting. They write that ineffective signs would be “pointless, but not harmful.” I disagree, for three reasons.

First, the essay overstates the evidence against signage. Its claim rests on two sources: a 2006 survey of Australian nurses and a 2008 commentary on the U.K.’s zero tolerance campaign. Neither evaluates whether signs change behavior. Nor has anyone tested the claim that signs erode patient trust. Research on violence prevention is methodologically weak, though multicomponent programs show mixed but encouraging results.

Second, deterrence is not the only purpose of a sign. Even if the signs prevented nothing, they would matter. The authors acknowledge that violence against health care workers has risen. In emergency departments specifically, it has introduced a mental and physical burden that fundamentally changes what it means to do this work. Signage stating an obvious standard of behavior may seem trivial, but it can have meaning as a piece of a broader institutional commitment to health care worker well-being. It tells staff that their hospital understands the realities of their work and supports them.

Third, signs remind clinicians where their obligations end. Many clinicians, especially trainees, are unsure what they owe a patient or family who is abusing them. A hospital must screen and stabilize anyone who comes to the emergency department, whatever their behavior. Beyond that, no patient or family is entitled to further care from a clinician they are making unsafe. This is easy to forget amid the pressures of clinical care, and a visible reminder helps.

The authors worry about the message these signs send to patients and families. That concern is thoughtful, but it is aimed at the wrong signs. The signs I see in emergency departments name physical assault, threats, and harassment. A sign that polices tone or discourages complaints could deserve criticism, but those signs are the exception and should not set the terms of this debate. Even the authors want their alternative signs to make clear that violence will not be tolerated. They close by asking that the words on hospital walls communicate medicine’s values. Protecting the people who care for patients is one of them.

— Joseph Shapiro, M.D., Children’s National Hospital

Torie Bosch is the First Opinion editor at STAT.

The smartest thinkers in life sciences on what's happening — and what's to come

Reporting from the frontiers of health and medicine

This Plug-In Hybrid SUV Towed A Boeing 747 On Only 2 Wheels. Yes, Really.

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

Chinese automakers are trying to capture as much of the European car market as possible, and they’re resorting to clever marketing tricks that legacy automakers have used in the past to attract buyers.

BYD is one of the many Chinese car companies trying to rack up orders, and its Denza brand has now recreated a famous Volkswagen commercial from two decades ago that involved a diesel-powered SUV and a jumbo jet. Only this time, Denza has an ace up its sleeve, and it’s quite impressive.

I vividly remember when I saw the original clip 20 years ago. A blue Volkswagen Touareg V10 diesel towed a 171-ton jet aircraft down the same Dunsfold runway in the United Kingdom where the old "Top Gear" episodes used to be filmed.

Now, the Denza B5 (or Bao 5 in some markets) plug-in hybrid SUV is back at the same place to do the same stunt, but adapted for a 2026 where everything seemingly needs to be bigger, more over the top, and more in-your-face. (I blame TikTok.)

So, we have a Chinese PHEV that uses two electric motors and a 1.5-liter gas engine. It’s all-wheel-drive, its 31.8-kilowatt-hour battery can deliver up to 56 miles of all-electric range on a full battery, and it has two mechanical locking differentials. This last part is important. The battery can be fast-charged at up to 100 kW, and the total system output is a healthy 536 horsepower, with most of that oomph being provided by the two e-motors.

We also have a 207-ton jumbo jet that got a quick repositioning for no other purpose than pure entertainment.

Here comes the interesting bit: While Volkswagen’s original stunt kept all four of the Touareg’s wheels in place—you know, how a car usually works—BYD decided to put its PHEV SUV to the test by gradually removing some wheels.

The first run was done with all four wheels in place, followed by a three-wheeled run with 2.2 tons of ballast. For the final act, both right wheels were removed, and the ballast reached 5 tonnes. During the three runs, the Denza B5 towed the Boeing 747-400 over one mile, and best of all, it used exclusively electric power.

The point of it all? Promote the new SUV to the UK market, where the Land Rover Defender is among its biggest rivals. Unlike the Defender, however, which is now based on a unibody design and is primarily combustion-powered, the Denza B5 sits on a bespoke ladder-frame chassis and more substantially electrified. It has a gas engine that acts mainly as a generator for the high-voltage battery. The battery cells are directly integrated between the chassis rails.

If you're thinking about trying this stunt at home, just know the vehicle needed a few modifications to pull it off. BYD added a custom towing attachment, removed some seats to make room for the ballast, and added stabilizers to make up for the missing wheels.

In this special report, William Ralston looks at the Seagulls’ chances of remaining in the mix, the secrets behind their success and the stats that bode very well for Fabian Hürzeler’s men in the long term.

Berrada used the word “stability” a lot in that interview. It’s a key buzzword at Manchester United who, although not traditionally a sacking club, have changed their manager mid-season for the past three campaigns in a row.

“My commitment is we’re going to do everything we can to put ourselves in a position to win titles consistently,” said Berrada. The Ineos project had 2028 down as the year United were going to win, or at least, challenge for the league.

Hmm … maybe push that back a few years, lads. Getting out of the relegation zone would be a start.

Back to Manchester United and it was interesting that Omar Berrada, the chief executive, gave a rare major interview yesterday. His chat with Sky Sports was aired just about Mauricio Pochettino’s first press conference.

These quotes, defending United’s summer transfer business, made for very interesting reading.

double quotation markWhen we look at the summer transfer window of 2026, I know we’re receiving a lot of criticism for it, but we’re actually happy with the players we bought.

We think that Carlos Baleba, Youri Tielemans, Andrey Santos, and Karl Darlow - and let’s not forget that we’ve also welcomed back Marcus Rashford, who had a very good season on loan at Barcelona - these are five additions that we think have made the squad fundamentally better.

When we find the right opportunities, we will spend. But what we don’t want to be doing is spending just for the sake of it. So, could we have signed an additional defender to straighten the depth and the quality? Yes, but we have to find the right players at the right value.

I think most United fans would bristle at Berrada claiming Rashford was a summer ‘addition’ … and the pressure is on for United to get things right in the next transfer windows, especially in defence.

double quotation markI don’t think you’re talking nonsense, but I’d say it’s tactical rather than a lack of talent. Look at Arsenal. Calafiori, Timber and White either bomb on or tuck into midfield, so the wide player has to work more like a No 8, working the half-space and tracking back, rather than being the classic ‘star man’ left high and wide to beat his full-back. Is Markel Group Stock a Buy After Falling 10% in 2026?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Markel stock has been surging in recent weeks.

By Dave Kovaleski – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:45AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Markel Group ›

After falling to a 52-week low of $1,704 per share on Oct. 1, Markel Group (MKL -0.77%) stock has rallied, rising about 15% to $1,952 per share (as of Nov. 19), though it’s still down about 10% for the year.

The specialty insurer and holding company operates a model that is not unlike Berkshire Hathaway (BRKB -0.36%). In fact, some have referred to it as a baby Berkshire Hathaway. Like Berkshire, Markel is mainly an insurance company that uses its float — the premiums it receives before paying out claims — to invest in a portfolio of stocks and privately owned businesses.

Markel has also not been as successful as Berkshire Hathaway, at least not in recent years. It has averaged a 9.2% annualized return during the past five years compared to Berkshire's 15.7% average return. During the past 10 years, Markel stock has posted an average return of 8.5% to 14.1% for Berkshire.

But like Berkshire, there is a lot to like about Markel.

You can't gauge Markel's quarter by looking at traditional earnings based on generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) because the unrealized investment gains and losses from its portfolio are included. Those unrealized gains or losses distort the actual performance of the company.

But if you look at its adjusted operating income, that shows how much the company made on the businesses it owns. In Q3, its adjusted operating income rose 14% from a year ago to $707 million, or $37.51 per share. That soundly beat analysts' estimates of $27.76 per share. Its revenue jumped 6% year-over-year to $4.16 billion, which easily beat estimates of $3.78 billion.

Within insurance, Markel saw its adjusted underwriting gross premium volume increase by 9% for the quarter. This excludes its global reinsurance division, which it exited in the past year, and its specialty car insurance business Hagerty, which transitioned to a fronting arrangement. Also, its net investment income increased 5% to $233 million.

The key result in insurance is the combined ratio, which measures how much the company makes in profits from premiums after claims are paid out and operating expenses are subtracted. Markel had a combined ratio of 92%, down from 93% a year ago.

For the full year, the combined ratio is 92%, down from 95% in 2026. This is a good thing because a combined ratio of more than 100 means you are paying out more than you are taking in. A ratio below 100 means you are making more than you are paying out. So, a combined ratio of 92% means Markel is making more profit per dollar of premiums than it was last year.

Markel's businesses, known as Markel Ventures, also performed well. Its industrial holdings saw operating income rise 29% year-over-year to $131 million, while its financial segment increased 30% to $80 million and its consumer properties rose 80% to $31 million.

Markel doesn't provide short-term projections, but its trends within insurance and its holdings are promising. The company is built to perform well in negative markets, which is a big reason the shares surged in recent weeks.

When investors make a flight to safety, they head for companies like Markel, which is grounded by its insurance holdings and conservative underwriting and its durable, value-oriented investments with low debt and stable earnings.

The stock is also fairly cheap right now, trading at less than 14 times earnings. I like it as a long-term buy, and it's particularly valuable now during bouts of market volatility.

Qatar’s Film Committee and Watermelon Pictures Forge Partnership to Co-Produce a Slate of Films From the ‘Global South’

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Qatar’s Film Committee and Watermelon Pictures have announced a strategic partnership to develop and co-produce a slate of films “championing visionary filmmakers and compelling stories from the Global South and beyond with an emphasis on culturally authentic storytelling and broad international appeal,” they said in a statement.

The Global South is a geopolitical and economic term that broadly includes countries in Africa, Latin America and the Caribbean, most of Asia, and most of Oceania. Related Stories Behind Election Night Scenes, Fox News Launches New Multimedia Era ‘GTA 6’ Upsets Conservatives: Fox News Tells Fans to ‘Grow Up’ as Clavicular and More Manosphere Influencers Complain About Lucia

The Qatar Film Committee is an official body that is part of the Media City Qatar hub and is tasked with driving growth of the country’s entertainment industry.

Founded in April 2024 by brothers Badie Ali and Hamza Ali, Chicago-based Watermelon Pictures has gained worldwide attention in its short time in business with releases such as Rashid Masharawi’s “From Ground Zero,” Annemarie Jacir’s “Palestine 36” and Cherien Dabis’ “All That’s Left of You,” which were shortlisted for the best international feature Oscar, as well as Kaouther ben Hania’s Oscar-nominated “The Voice of Hind Rajab.”

The partnership between Qatar’s Film Committee and Watermelon was announced on the second day of the Doha Film Festival’s Industry Days, which are running from Nov. 19-21.

The plan is for the pact to spawn a slate of projects developed by Watermelon spanning comedy, television, genre and drama. Financial terms were not disclosed.

“Combining Qatar’s commitment to world-class filmmaking, growing production capabilities, and investment in the creative economy with Watermelon’s access to leading talent, established distribution infrastructure, and deep understanding of what resonates with audiences, the collaboration aims to produce commercially compelling films with high production values and international appeal,” the statement said.

The plan envisions that these projects will shoot mainly in Qatar or be structured as Qatari co-productions. They will tap into the country’s growing production ecosystem and the Qatar Screen Production Incentive rebate offering up to 50% in cash, “creating opportunities for local crews, businesses and creative talent while strengthening Qatar’s position as an international production hub,” it noted.

“Through this partnership, we want to give filmmakers the resources to tell stories rooted in their communities and bring them to audiences around the world,” Hassan Al Thawadi, the Qatari lawyer who chairs the Qatar Film Committee, said in the statement.

For Watermelon Pictures, the partnership marks a significant leap into larger-scale, mainstream production, backed by its distribution expertise.

Bayern Munich confirm Harry Kane surgery as England captain issues statement from hospital bed

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

England captain Harry Kane has undergone successful surgery on an ankle ligament injury which is expected to keep him sidelined for three months.

The recently crowned Ballon d’Or winner started for Bayern Munich as they drew with Cologne on Friday evening, but was forced off after just 20 minutes.

He was caught in a late tackle by Joel Schmied as he looked to latch onto a Michael Olise cross and immediately went down clutching his lower leg. He attempted to play on, but after spending a few minutes as a virtual bystander, he signalled to the bench that he could not continue.

It was confirmed at full-time he had suffered a tear to the syndesmotic ligament in his ankle, with Bayern head coach Vincent Kompany predicting he would miss around three months.

And on Saturday morning the club confirmed he had undergone successful surgery on the injury, with the ball now rolling on a lengthy period of rehabilitation.

Kane later shared a photo of himself on the edge of a hospital bed, bearing a broad grin and brandishing two thumbs-ups despite wearing a protective boot on his left foot.

He wrote: “The highs and lows of life! Obviously very disappointed to get this injury but as always it’s an opportunity to show resilience and improvement going into 2027. I’ll be back fresh and ready to go for the business end of the season!

“‘Tough times don’t last, tough people do’ - my guy [NFL legend Julian Edelman] told me this one!

“Thank you for all the messages! Alles gut!”

Ankle injuries plagued Kane throughout his 14-year stint at Tottenham, but he has largely managed to stay fit since moving to Munich in 2023.

It remains to be seen whether Kane will be fit in time to lead England out in the Nations League quarter-finals in March 2027. The two legs of that tie will be played on March 27 and 30.

The projected three-month rehabilitation period suggests he will be back in contention in late February, and Thomas Tuchel will hope he can get back fit and firing in time to don the captain’s armband in Oslo.

Kane has been fielding plenty of well wishes. England’s official Instagram account replied to his post confirming his succesful surgery, writing in a comment: “Wishing you a speedy recovery, Harry!”

Chelsea and England forward Morgan Rogers has also joined Joshua Kimmich, Manuel Neuer and Dayot Upamecano in sending their support to the 33-year-old.

Social Security's Trump Bump-Led 2027 COLA Is the 5th Largest in 35 Years -- but There's a Dark Side to This Outsize Increase as Well

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Though Social Security checks are about to get bigger, the projected timeline for sweeping benefit cuts may have accelerated.

By Sean Williams – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:36AM EST

For Social Security’s nearly 72 million traditional beneficiaries -- retired workers, workers with disabilities, and survivors of deceased workers -- there are few, if any, announcements more anticipated than the annual cost-of-living adjustment (COLA).

Social Security's COLA is the near-annual "raise" given to beneficiaries to account for inflation (i.e., rising prices). If the price for the goods and services that seniors regularly purchase kept climbing without an adjustment in benefits, the buying power of Social Security income would steadily decline over time.

Social Security's announced 2027 COLA offers something of a mixed bag for recipients. On the one hand, it's set to deliver one of the largest raises over the last 35 years. This larger payout comes courtesy of President Donald Trump and a handful of his policies.

President Trump's policies offer a good-news/bad-news scenario for Social Security. Image source: Official White House Photo by Molly Riley.

But this outsize Trump bump isn't without its consequences. Larger payouts in the new year can make the program's precarious financial outlook that much worse.

On Oct. 14, the Social Security Administration announced that monthly payouts would increase by 3.6% in 2027. For the average retired-worker beneficiary, their monthly payout is expected to climb by $75. Meanwhile, the typical worker with a disability can expect their monthly Social Security check to rise by $59 in the new year.

A 3.6% raise is well above the norm. It marks a tie for the fifth-largest percentage increase over the last 35 years, surpassed only by COLAs of 4.1% (2006), 5.8% (2009), 5.9% (2022), and 8.7% (2023).

It's also the sixth consecutive year in which recipients have received at least a 2.5% cost-of-living adjustment. It's been 30 years (1988-1997) since benefits rose by at least 2.5% for six straight years.

While modest inflation associated with a growing U.S. economy results in a Social Security raise most years, next year's outsize COLA was pumped up by two of President Donald Trump's policies.

New from the New York Fed: "By February 2026, tariffs had contributed 2.9 pp to goods price inflation, and without them goods prices would have fallen slightly." About one quarter of every point in higher tariff rates shows up in consumer prices within one year. • A 10% across-the-board tariff yields a 2.6% rise in consumer goods prices after one year. • About two thirds of the increase comes from pricier imports, which get passed through quickly. • Another third comes from U.S. made goods, as producers face higher input costs and less import competition. This takes longer, about 6-12 months, to filter through.

To begin with, each of the last two Social Security COLAs has been influenced by Trump's tariff and trade policy. Adding duties to imports directly raises their price and can increase manufacturing costs for domestic producers.

A recently published report by three New York Federal Reserve economists writing for Liberty Street Economics ("How Fast Do Tariffs Pass Through into Consumer Prices?") found that Trump's 2025 tariffs increased goods price inflation by an estimated 2.9 percentage points by February 2026. Without tariffs, the NY Fed economists estimate that goods prices would have otherwise fallen.

The other major policy contributing to Social Security's 2027 Trump bump is the Iran war. Closing the Strait of Hormuz has resulted in the largest energy supply disruption in modern history, sending gas and diesel prices notably higher.

With Trumpflation (inflation driven by Trump's policies) becoming entrenched in the U.S. economy, it's quite possible that these policies provide a third consecutive year with a Trump bump in 2028.

Unfortunately, outsize Social Security COLAs come at a potentially steep cost to America's leading retirement program.

Every year since 1985, the Social Security Board of Trustees Report has warned of a long-term unfunded obligation. In simpler terms, the projected income to be collected in the 75 years following the release of a report is expected to be insufficient to cover outlays (primarily benefits, but also the administrative costs to oversee Social Security). As of 2026, this long-term unfunded obligation had ballooned to $29.3 trillion.

However, this isn't the most pressing concern for the program or its nearly 72 million traditional beneficiaries.

According to the latest Trustees Report, the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance trust fund (OASI) is forecast to exhaust its asset reserves by the fourth quarter of 2032. The OASI's asset reserves represent the excess income collected since inception that is invested in special-issue, interest-bearing government bonds, as required by law.

US Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund Assets at End of Year data by YCharts.

The positive news is that Social Security isn't going bankrupt and is in no danger of halting benefits. More than 91% of the program's income is collected through the 12.4% payroll tax on earned income (wages and salaries, but not investment income). As long as Americans keep working, the payroll tax will continue generating income for disbursement to eligible beneficiaries... even if the OASI's asset reserves are depleted.

But just because Social Security isn't going bankrupt, it doesn't mean everything is peachy. If the OASI's asset reserves run dry over the next six years, as predicted, it would indicate that the current payout schedule, inclusive of annual COLAs, isn't sustainable. Sweeping benefit cuts of up to 22% may be necessary for retired workers and survivors of deceased workers.

The concern is that Social Security's Trump bump may have materially altered the trajectory to the OASI's asset reserve insolvency.

When the Trustees Report issues its short- and long-term outlooks, it takes into account a long list of variables, including birth rates, death rates, net migration rates, and even annual COLAs. The Trustees assume that annual COLAs will be modest -- but as we just saw, a 3.6% annual raise is anything but average.

Social Security's outsize 2027 raise risks draining the OASI even faster than initially forecast.

President Trump's "Big, Beautiful Bill" already moved up the projected OASI asset reserve insolvency timeline by one quarter (from the first quarter of 2033 to the fourth quarter of 2032). It's entirely possible the 2027 Trump bump further accelerates the timeline to sweeping benefit cuts.

Rosalía Takes the Statement Hat Trend to the Skies

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Is it a bird? Is it a plane? No, it’s Rosalía, wearing one of the most spectacular headpieces of the season.

On Friday night, the Spanish singer arrived at the LOS40 Music Awards in Barcelona with an enormous white dove perched above her head, its wings dramatically outstretched as though caught mid-flight. The sculptural creation came courtesy of Robert Wun’s fall 2026 couture collection, and was paired with a custom ivory Maison Margiela gown, featuring a plunging halter neckline and delicate pleating. With her dark hair pulled back and her bleached blonde halo, the avian accessory was given plenty of room to spread its wings.

It’s a particularly pertinent choice of chapeau for the singer, whose Lux era has been steeped in religious imagery and otherworldly fashion. The dove, of course, is a traditional symbol of peace and the Holy Spirit. Rosalía has already experimented with all manner of heavenly headwear, from the Margiela nun-inspired headpiece on her album cover to the theatrical hats worn throughout her Lux tour. This might be her most divine accessory yet.

As it happens, the dove has been rather busy lately. Just days earlier, Whitney Peak wore a cobalt-blue gown from the same Robert Wun collection to the New York premiere of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping. Her dress featured two sculptural white birds appearing to suspend the fabric in midair. Wun, whose fantastical couture designs regularly blur the line between fashion, costume, and sculpture, is becoming something of a go-to for stars seeking genuinely captivating red-carpet creations.

Consider Elle Fanning, who recently embraced the gloriously eccentric style of her Hunger Games character Effie Trinket in a pink Robert Wun couture gown, complete with a matching pillbox hat topped by a miniature ballerina. Fanning has also worn a saucy red satin creation by British milliner Rachel Trevor-Morgan.

And she’s in excellent company. Dua Lipa recently championed the artsy chapeau in a frayed Chanel couture number, while Anya Taylor-Joy opted for a gigantic pink Jacquemus hat that framed her head like a halo. Even winter’s more practical accessories have taken a more eccentric turn: Hunter Schafer has been championing furry, archival Undercover headgear, while Taylor Russell has collaborated with Ilana Blumberg on some particularly fabulous fuzzy hats.

For Wun, the theatricality is very much the point. His fall 2026 couture collection, titled Child's Play, explored childhood and fairytales through a procession of fantastical characters, from Snow White to the Big Bad Wolf. Working with milliner Awon Golding, the designer produced a series of elaborate hats and sculptural headpieces that seemed to defy the laws of gravity.

After seasons of slicked-back buns and minimalist accessories, fashion’s most adventurous dressers are evidently ready for something a little more playful. The message? There’s no such thing as too much drama up top. And as Rosalía has demonstrated, sometimes the best accessories have a life of their own—and take flight.
