Why Are Bitcoin, Ethereum and XRP Prices Still Falling and What’s Next?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Bitcoin fell to $61,496.58, down 0.7% in 24 hours and 11.5% over the past week. Ether dropped 1.3% on the day to $1,724.13 and is down 12.3% for the week. XRP held up better than both, edging 0.3% higher to $0.9593. It has lost 5.5% over seven days.

A short rebound earlier in the week ran out of steam at resistance, and sellers took control again.

Bitcoin climbed off a low near $62,000 last week but stalled in the $65,000 to $67,000 zone. That band had worked as support before the crash, and once broken it turned into resistance. Rejections there were expected, and the price has since slid back toward its lows.

According to analyst Josh of Crypto World, several factors are still weighing on the market:

At $61,496, Bitcoin is back inside the $61,000 to $62,100 support zone, where it bounced last week. This is the most important level on the chart in the near term.

The base case calls for choppy sideways trading, with Bitcoin holding above its recent low for a week or two rather than staging a sharp recovery. Even a move above $67,000 would not guarantee a quick run to $74,000 while momentum stays weak.

Ether has fallen to $1,724, just below the bottom of its $1,730 to $1,800 support zone. Several tests of this area in recent days had failed to produce a daily close below it. A daily close under $1,730 would be the first confirmed break.

The daily RSI had started to recover from oversold levels, pointing to a short pause in the crash. That recovery is now being tested.

If the break below $1,730 holds, Ether could slide toward $1,650 sooner than expected.

XRP Holds Up Better but Faces a Wall at $1

XRP is the strongest of the three this week. The token climbed toward the $0.99 to $1.02 resistance zone but struggled there, as expected, and has eased back to about $0.96.

The daily RSI is close to leaving oversold territory, which leaves a little room for a further mild bounce.

Busan-Selected Director Anoop Lokkur Turns His Own Tenant War Into Bengaluru Dark Comedy ‘Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane’ at India’s WAVES Film Bazaar

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Indian-Australian filmmaker Anoop Lokkur, whose debut “Don’t Tell Mother” premiered at the Busan International Film Festival in 2025, is turning his own standoff with tenants who wouldn’t leave his family home into “Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane” (Upstairs Downstairs), a dark comedy in the Co-Production Market at WAVES Film Bazaar.

The Kannada and English-language film follows Karthik, who returns from Melbourne, along with his wife and young daughter, to look after his ailing father. He expects to take over the top floor of the house in an older Bengaluru neighborhood where his family has lived for three generations. The tenant, government contractor Ramesh, had agreed to move out, then changes his mind. Polite negotiation gives way to a tit-for-tat war as legal routes go nowhere, the power gets cut, property is damaged and access is blocked. Related Stories 'Jeopardy!' Sets Inaugural Fan Convention in Washington, D.C. Dean Koontz's Jane Hawk Books to Be Developed as TV Series by Monarch Media (EXCLUSIVE)

Lokkur says that in 2025 he returned to Bangalore with his wife and their 1-year-old daughter, planning to settle upstairs in his parents’ home, but the tenants stayed on after their lease ran out and the family ended up crammed into his parents’ part of the house. “The whole situation was frustrating and unpleasant. What followed were months of tension, passive aggression and even thinking of ways to get them to vacate. The irony, though, was that only a couple of years earlier, my wife and I had been forced out of a rented home ourselves.”

Having been on both sides of the door, he says, he grew curious about ownership, about what it means to call a place home and about how hard people will push to come out on top. “I realized there was a dark comedy in this situation that was universal, and the best way to deal with my frustration was to turn it into a film.”

The project is in development, with Lokkur and Mikayla Henke producing through Papunu Films. The budget is $1.3 million, with $156,000 in place, and the team is looking for international co-producers, financiers and sales agents in Goa.

“The film is built like a tennis match. Your sympathies keep switching between the two families, until you’re no longer sure who to cheer for,” Lokkur says. “I’m interested in how ego quietly takes over and how ordinary decent people justify each small act of revenge until they no longer recognize themselves. It’s also a film about Bangalore, a city changing so fast that old neighborhoods and family homes are disappearing.”

Henke says she went through the dispute alongside Lokkur, and that with the family living it every day, it was the obvious choice for their next film. “‘Don’t Tell Mother’ was a tender and very personal film. On the surface, ‘Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane’ (Upstairs Downstairs) might look like the opposite: darker, bolder and funnier. But it comes from the same place, a close eye on family and the things people struggle to say to each other. This time, we just want to take audiences on a much wilder ride.”

“Don’t Tell Mother” played in the A Window on Asian Cinema section at Busan, was nominated for best youth film at the 2026 Asia Pacific Screen Awards and had its Australian premiere at this year’s Sydney Film Festival. Lokkur’s short “Long Distance,” which Henke produced, screened at the Melbourne International Film Festival and Palm Springs International ShortFest before Canal+ acquired it. Henke was also the first producer on “Don’t Tell Mother.”

“While it’s set in Bengaluru, it’s a universal story about housing that people everywhere can relate to and we believe it can travel well beyond India,” the producers say.

WAVES Film Bazaar runs as the market component of the International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa.

Christie’s tepid 21st-century sale caps week of blockbuster auctions in New York

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Christie's auctioneer Yü-Ge Wang sells the top lot of the sale auction house's 19 November auction in New York, Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Untitled (Leonardo and His Five Grotesque Heads) (1983) Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Wrapping up this autumn’s week of marquee evening sales in New York, Christie’s 21st-century auction on Thursday (19 November) proved rather lacklustre, bringing in $117.7m ($145.9m with fees). That tally fell short of Christie’s pre-sale expectations, which do not account for fees, and projected a total take in the range of $126m to $192m. It was also a lower result compared to the auction house’s last two equivalent sales in New York—namely the $132m ($162.6m with fees) generated across 42 lots last May, and the $99.5m ($123.5m with fees) brought by 45 lots one year ago.

Just five of the 56 lots offered Thursday night failed to sell, for a slim buy-in rate of 9%. Two lots were withdrawn at the last moment, including a Jeff Koons sculpture estimated to bring between $3m and $5m. Twenty-four lots were backed by third-party guarantees and nine were backed fully by house guarantees, representing around 59% of all the works offered. Auction records for three artists were broken over the course of the night.

Roy Lichtenstein, Small Wall Explosion, 1965 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Business began with a bang at Christie’s Rockefeller Center salesroom: the first lot was Roy Lichtenstein’s Small Wall Explosion (1965), executed in porcelain enamel on steel and perforated steel relief to mimic the Ben-Day dot style from his paintings. It sold to a telephone bidder for $625,000 ($800,000 with fees). From an edition of six, it hailed from the collection of Antonio Homem Sonnabend, the adopted son of the late and legendary dealer and collector Ileana Sonnabend (1914-2007) and who ran his mother’s namesake gallery in its waning years. Antonio Homem died earlier this year, at age 86.

Another of the 19 Sonnabend lots from Sonnabend, Ashley Bickerton’s expansive wall relief Commercial Piece #3 (1990), covered with gallery and fashion-house logos and comprised of anodised aluminium, wood, leather, acrylic and rubber, sold for $120,000 ($153,600 with fees). Both the Lichtenstein and the Bickerton were backed by third-party guarantees.

Andy Warhol, Campbell's Soup Can (Turkey Noodle), 1962 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Price points for the Sonnabend trove zoomed higher with Andy Warhol’s iconicand hand painted Campbell’s Soup Can (Turkey Noodle) (1962), which sold for $7.3m ($9m with fees) against an estimate of $6m to $8m. Another Lichtenstein, the painting Large Spool (1963)—a razor-sharp depiction of the titular object that has been featured in multiple gallery and museum exhibitions—squeaked by on the tails of a third-party guarantee at $7m ($8.6m with fees) against an ambitious estimate of $10m to $15m.

Still in the Sonnabend lots, Jeff Koons’s toy-like Hulk (Friends) (2004-12), in polychromed bronze and standing nearly 6ft tall, found a new home for $3.3m ($4.1m with fees). And another Warhol, redolent with Hollywood celebrity, The Kiss (Bela Lugosi) (1963) in silkscreen ink on white paper, sold to another telephone bidder for $3.3m ($4.6m with fees).

A petite Jasper Johns oil painting on cardboard, Numbers (1957), measuring just 8.5in by 6in and rich in shades of grey, made $2.6m ($3.2m with fees). It was featured in the artist’s first solo exhibition at the Leo Castelli Gallery in 1958. Ileana Sonnabend acquired it from the Castelli Gallery in 1959, the same year of her divorce from Leo Castelli.

The Sonnabend group of 19 lots, including three examples of important 20th-century French decorative art—among them a Jean Prouvé Trapéze table from around 1956 that hit $1.3m ($1.6m with fees)—all in tallied $33.3m ($41.9m with fees), compared to Christie’s pre-sale expectations for the group of $35m to $55m.

Illustrious consignor names continued with works from the collection of the late Museum of Modern Art trustee and patron Agnes Gund sold to benefit the Gund Art Foundation. They included Robert Ryman’s immaculate, small, square all-over white composition Untitled (1961), which brought $2.9m ($3.6m with fees). Vija Celmins’s photorealist oil painting Untitled (Ocean) (1990), which featured in the San Francisco Museum of Modern Art’s traveling retrospective in 2018-2020, went for $5.7m ($7.1m with fees) against an estimate of $6m to $8m. It also was backed by a third-party guarantee.

Vija Celmins, Untitled (Ocean), 1990 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Gund acquired Alexander Calder’s hanging metal mobile The Eye in the Black (1963) from the Perls Galleries in New York in in 1967. On Thursday night it went to an online bidder for $2.8m ($3.5m with fees). Cindy Sherman’s Untitled Film Still #5 (1977), also widely exhibited and written about by major critics, sold for $320,000 ($409,6000 with fees). Gund had acquired it at Sotheby’s New York in November 2000 for a hammer price of $75,000.

Gund’s contemporary taste bordered on the encyclopaedic, as evidenced by an early Robert Rauschenberg work, Untitled (Gold Painting) (1953), in gold leaf on fabric and paper collage on canvas. The diminutive work, at 8in by 8in, fetched a sizeable sum, $1.7m ($2.1m with fees). Gund acquired it at Sotheby’s in New York in May 1994 for a $55,000 hammer price.

A modestly scaled Lichtenstein work on paper in coloured pencil, graphite and paper collage, Artist’s Studio with Model (Study) (1974), sold to the art adviser Ralph DeLuca for $950,000 ($1.2m with fees).

Jim Nutt, Loom, 1999 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

A standout from the Gund trove, a work she described as “my Mona Lisa”, was another modestly-scaled work, by Jim Nutt, founder of Chicago’s Hairy Who group. Titled Loom (1999) and measuring 15in by 14in, the portrait of a woman with a penetrating gaze set a new auction record for Nutt’s work when it more than doubled its high estimate of $350,000 and ultimately sold for $850,000 ($1m with fees). The ten lots sold to benefit Gund’s charitable foundation tallied $20.3m ($25.7m with fees), squarely within Christie’s pre-sale estimate of $16m to $24m.

Though not part of the Gund Art Foundation offerings, Alice Neel’s double portrait David and Catherine Saalfield (1982) hailed from Agnes Gund’s family and brought $1.6m ($2m with fees). Gund acquired it directly from the artist the year it was painted—not surprising since the sitters were her young adult children.

Alice Neel, David and Catherine Saalfield, 1982 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

The appeal of buying a piece of the legacy of storied dealers and collectors did not fare so well for a work that had belonged to the late gallerist Marian Goodman. Bidding on Gerhard Richter’s Abstraktes Bild (1990) stalled at $5.7m, against an estimate of $6m to $8m, and the lot failed to sell. As Richter’s longtime New York dealer, Goodman acquired the lushly surfaced squeegee painting directly from the artist and exhibited the painting in a gallery group show the year it was made. (A second Richter from another consignor fared better, with that somewhat larger Abstraktes Bild from 1991 bringing $7.6m, or $9.4m with fees.)

Among the works from more anonymous consignors, Richard Prince’s famous Nurse series made a splash when Everglade Nurse (2003), executed in acrylic and inkjet print on canvas, sold for $3.1m ($3.9m with fees). It last sold at Sotheby’s New York in November 2008 for $3.4m with fees, a rather flat return after 18 years.

Large-scale paintings destined to test living room wall space were also in evidence with Christopher Wool’s untitled black-and-white abstraction from 2009, measuring more than 8ft tall. It brought $2m ($2.5m with fees). George Condo’s more than 9ft-wide Double Heads in Blue and Silver (2014), realised $2.4m ($3m with fees). The Condo hailed from the collection of Stefan Edlis and his wife Gael Neeson, who donated 44 works cumulatively valued at more than $400m to the Art Institute of Chicago in 2015.

The top lot of the evening, unsurprisingly, was Jean-Michel Basquiat’s quirky and text-rich composition, Untitled (Leonardo and his Five Grotesque Heads) (1983), which hammered to another anonymous telephone bidder for $28m ($32.8m with fees) against a sky-high estimate of $30m to $50m.

Jean-Michel Basquiat, Untitled (Leonardo and His Five Grotesque Heads), 1983 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Evidently inspired by Leonardo da Vinci’s 1493 pen-and-ink drawing on paper A man tricked by Gypsies (also known as Five grotesque heads), from the Royal Collection Trust at Windsor Castle in London, Basquiat’s freestyle interpretation in oil stick and acrylic against a burnt orange background crackles with the artist’s terse way with wordplay and actually features eight heads in all.

Measuring 80in by 80in, the painting has no exhibition history, at least none acknowledged in Christie’s online catalogue entry—though it was included in the Whitney Museum of American Art’s 1992 Basquiat retrospective. The catalogue entry for the Whitney exhibition listed the owner as Zurich’s Bruno Bischofberger, an early and important champion of the artist. It last sold at auction at Sotheby’s London in October 2005, when it was the top lot at £736,000 ($1.3m) including fees against an estimate of £400,000 to £600,000. Sotheby’s at the time identified the buyer as “US Trade” and the current consignor acquired it from that entity in 2006.

Julia Jo, One Fell Swoop, 2026 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

The evening ended on a sweet note with Julia Jo’s large-scale abstraction One Fell Swoop (2026), donated to benefit the New York-based Coalition for the Homeless. It set a new auction record for Jo’s work, fetching $320,000 ($409,600 with fees). (The night’s other new auction record was for the work of the Spanish artist Antonio Santín.)

Apart from that feeling, the outlook was not as rosy. “It’s been a very long week, with a lot of art, and I think people are tired,” DeLuca said. “The theme of the week is that estimates are a little aspirational.”

The long week’s last burst of activity will be a pair of day sales at Christie’s on Friday (20 November), after which the US art industry will mark a pause to observe Thanksgiving before migrating south for Art Basel Miami Beach.

With estates and collections accounting for some of the season’s biggest consignments, auction houses are betting wealthy collectors will shrug off market volatility

Despite the underwhelming result, the sale notched new auction records for Barkley Hendricks and Alma Thomas

New auction records were set for Pollock, Rothko, Brancusi, Miro and Neel

Swan song of veteran auctioneer Jussi Pylkkänen made $640.8m—the house's highest total for a non-single-owner sale in six years

$500 Invested in GE Aerospace (GE) Stock Today Could Be Worth This Much in 3 Years

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The stock has been volatile, but its price is appealing, and its growth prospects good.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 20, 2026 at 2:05AM EST

10 stocks we like better than GE Aerospace ›

So you're thinking of investing $500 in GE Aerospace (GE -2.81%) and you're wondering what kind of growth you can expect over the coming years. Well, no one can really know just how a stock will perform in the future -- though we can make some educated guesses about it.

Let's take a look at GE Aerospace, which leads in designing, building, and servicing aircraft turbine engines. It recently boasted an installed base of approximately 50,000 commercial and 30,000 military aircraft engines.

Here's how the stock has grown over time:

Data source: Morningstar.com, as of Nov. 17, 2026.

Looking at the table, it's hard to guess how much it will grow in three years. One rough approach might be to take its longest-term average of close to 9% and boost it a bit, conservatively, perhaps to 11%. If the stock grows by 11% annually for three years, it will grow by about 37%. So from the recent stock price of $329, it could reach $450 per share. If you spent $500 on the stock (buying fractional shares), your stake would grow to $684.

But should you buy into the stock? Well, the recent stock price of $329 is down about 15% from its 52-week high to of $389. Still, its shares look somewhat overvalued, with a recent price-to-sales ratio of 6.5, well above the five-year average of 2.2, and its forward-looking price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 35 is a bit above the five-year average of 32. If you buy now and plan to hang on for many years, you could do well. But if you hold out for (and snag) a lower entry point, you could do even better.

There's a lot to like about GE Aerospace, such as its $170 backlog of commercial services and its third-quarter year-over-year revenue jump of 49%, with profit margins rising, too.

Daniel Farke already has Ethan Ampadu 2.0, but Leeds fans must wait to see him

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Senior Correspondent AUTHORITY Senior football journalist specialising in refereeing and officiating; former contributor to The Football League Paper, Late Tackle, and the Premier League; University of Derby graduate. FOCUS Refereeing controversies, football finance and governance, PGMOL decisions, and officiating analysis across the Premier League, EFL, and SPFL. THE INSIGHT Harri utilises a network of officiating and industry contacts to deliver verified, expert analysis of refereeing decisions and PGMOL policy. He provides the regulatory clarity behind the controversy to ensure fans get the full picture.

Ethan Ampadu has become instrumental in how Leeds United play under Daniel Farke.

Since joining the Whites in July 2023, the Welshman has worked his way up the pecking order, earning the captain's armband at Elland Road.

He has become one of the most consistent players at Farke's disposal, whether he's deployed the middle of the park of the centre of defence.

Ampadu already has 138 appearances for Leeds, and he can do just about everything, everything but shoot accurately, many would say.

If Farke could have a whole team of Ampadu's, he'd likely take that option.

He's a prime example of what the youngsters at Thorp Arch should be striving to be, and Farke may already have another talent at his disposal.

Jayden Lienou is a name that an increasing number of supporters are becoming aware of, with multiple appearances on the first-team bench.

The 18-year-old Welshman is also involved with Craig Bellamy's Wales team, but in both, he's yet to be given his debut by either manager.

Ampadu was forced to seek several loan deals before landing on his feet at Elland Road, and history might be repeating itself.

According to Tom Collomosse, several Championship clubs are keen on signing Lienou on loan in the January window, leaving Leeds with a decision to make.

New: Leeds v excited about Jayden Lienou, 18yo left-back in full Wales squad. Lienou has been training with first team since Feb and featured in summer. Several Champ clubs interested in possible loan in Jan. Decision 🔜 #lufc

Of course, he's naturally a left-back, but he's also capable of featuring at centre-back. At his age, it wouldn't be impossible to mould him into a midfielder.

Lienou would definitely benefit from a loan move in January, but it remains to be seen if those at Thorp Arch also agree.

Mick Brown has told Football Insider that Dan James could push to leave Leeds, though Farke is not open to that, it is believed.

Jaka Bijol's future has been a big talking point, and the same can be said about Ilia Gruev and Lukas Nmecha, who are struggling for minutes.

Football Insider understands that Nmecha's most likely destination is the Bundesliga, returning to Germany for the first time since July 2025.

Besiktas are open to offloading Vaclav Cerny in the new year amid reports of Rangers' renewed interest in his services, sources have told Football Insider.

Cerny impressed during a season-long loan spell at Rangers in 2024-25 after he had fallen out of favour at Wolfsburg.

He started this season strongly, with four goals and an assist in his first 12 appearances, but reports of an exit have increased in recent weeks, as he has only notched one assist in his previous seven outings.

Gers fans were keen to see Cerny re-sign in the summer, and the same is likely to apply now as they aim to finally topple Celtic in the Premiership once again.

Club hero and ex-boss Barry Ferguson insisted in August that the Czech international's signature should have been a priority ahead of the new season.

Speaking via Go Radio, he said: "Yeah, no-brainer, if a deal can be done. He's a difference-maker, he's a top professional.

"He understands Glasgow, he understands what goes on here. He's been here before and he's a quality operator, so he ticks all the boxes for me."

BigLaw boosts bonuses, pay for associates creating new market rate following Cravath, Milbank hikes

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Major U.S. law firms are giving associates raises and establishing a new market rate ranging from $235,000 for first-year lawyers to $455,000 for senior associates, plus additional bonuses. (Image from Shutterstock)

Major U.S. law firms are giving associates raises and establishing a new market rate ranging from $235,000 for first-year lawyers to $455,000 for senior associates, plus additional bonuses.

Cravath, Swaine & Moore’s associate salary scale was increased Tuesday, and it rivals the raises announced in June at Milbank, according to coverage by Reuters.

Milbank increased the associate salary range from $235,000 to $455,000. In July, it offered special bonuses between $6,000 and $25,000 for associates. Reuters is also reporting pay increases at Fried, Frank, Harris, Shriver & Jacobson and Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton.

Cravath’s special bonuses—$11,000 to $40,000—based on class year, exceeded Milbank’s scale. It’s year-end bonus scale starts at $15,000 and extends to $115,000, which is a continuation of what the firm and others offered in past years.

Iran’s ‘Boiling Frog’ Strategy

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Iran’s ‘Boiling Frog’ Strategy

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

No audio? Hover over the video player, and tap the Click to Unmute button. Subtitles are also available. Closed captioning provided by Vimeo and may contain minor inconsistencies.

Register now to watch this on-demand recording.

One of many ways to interpret the U.S. midterm results is that they were an opportunity for Americans to express their concerns about the war in Iran. In that reading of events, Tehran’s strategy to hurt U.S. President Donald Trump has had at least some impact. But now that the elections are over, what will change? Will Trump feel like he now has no electoral constraints, widening his range of options in the war? And will Tehran feel like it has lost some leverage?

According to Vali Nasr, a longtime Iran watcher, Iranian leaders believe that sustained disruptions to the global economy serve as a lever to change the actions of Western leaders—and perhaps act as a future deterrent. “Iran sees the result of the U.S. elections as proof of this concept. Their strategy is to boil the frog gradually,” he said on the latest episode of FP Live.

One of many ways to interpret the U.S. midterm results is that they were an opportunity for Americans to express their concerns about the war in Iran. In that reading of events, Tehran’s strategy to hurt U.S. President Donald Trump has had at least some impact. But now that the elections are over, what will change? Will Trump feel like he now has no electoral constraints, widening his range of options in the war? And will Tehran feel like it has lost some leverage?

According to Vali Nasr, a longtime Iran watcher, Iranian leaders believe that sustained disruptions to the global economy serve as a lever to change the actions of Western leaders—and perhaps act as a future deterrent. “Iran sees the result of the U.S. elections as proof of this concept. Their strategy is to boil the frog gradually,” he said on the latest episode of FP Live.

Nasr is a professor and former dean of the School of Advanced International Studies at Johns Hopkins University. He is the author of, most recently, Iran’s Grand Strategy. The full interview is available on the video box atop this page or on the FP Live podcast on Spotify, YouTube, or Apple. What follows here is a lightly edited transcript.

Ravi Agrawal: Let’s start with the U.S. point of view on the Iran war. The consensus has been that Trump has three options. He could escalate militarily, but that has very uncertain outcomes. He could spin a victory and get out, but the spin is . Or he could focus on diplomacy and try to return to the memorandum of understanding (MOU) we had last summer, but that is a lot of hard work. What do you think the White House strategy is now that the midterms are over?

Vali Nasr: I think the president is actually pursuing all three strategies at the same time. Leaning in on diplomacy does not mean that he’s setting aside war plans, and asking for war plans does not mean that he is doing less diplomacy. He doesn’t have any good choices.

Ideally, Trump would end this war through a negotiated settlement, much like what happened in June, but he has to meet the Iranian price for doing that, which is actually quite steep for him. Currently, the positions of the two are too far apart. The Iranians want him to first go back to the MOU and implement it before they talk about anything else, such as the nuclear issue. Trump, on the other hand, needs the nuclear issue to be at the forefront in order to justify why he went to war for nine months.

War is also not really an option, because in order for war to be meaningful, Trump has to do something big. But something big opens the door to Iranian retaliation, particularly in the Persian Gulf, which could devastate the global economy in larger ways. I’m sure Trump is hearing from Chinese President Xi Jinping and Arab, Chinese, and Russian leaders that nobody wants that kind of damage to the global economy. So he’s settling for the third option, which is to let Iran sit under the blockade and do nothing. In my opinion, that’s not a strategy. That’s twiddling his thumbs.

The blockade is not foolproof. Iran is getting oil out and making money. At the same time, the Strait of Hormuz is still under a lot of pressure. The Iranians have actually increased the number of attacks they’re making there. Furthermore, I don’t think it’s sustainable, financially or otherwise, for the U.S. Navy to maintain the blockade long term. It is quite financially and labor intensive for the Navy to do so, and it doesn’t have any bases in the Persian Gulf that could provide relief. If the Iranians are able to sustain themselves with the amount of holes that already exist, then, ultimately, this is not sustainable.

In fact, if the blockade starts working in the way in which the U.S. treasury secretary and U.S. secretary of state say it will, then Iran is likely to escalate. So ultimately, this strategy that everybody thinks is settled on is not tenable in the long term. Either it will collapse under its own weight, or one or the other party is bound to disturb it, which might bring us back to war.

RA: I imagine that you could have said a lot of this two weeks ago. The big change that has come in the last couple of weeks is, of course, the U.S. midterm election results. Come January, the Democrats will have power in both the House of Representatives and the Senate. They feel that part of the mandate from the American people is trying to find a way to end the war. What is your sense of how Congress might impact how Trump tries to prosecute the end of this war, or dilly-dally, as you said?

VN: One outcome of these elections is a much more visible push within the United States against the war. It’s not just that the American people have spoken. If there was any shadow of a doubt that they really, really disliked this war, it has been dashed, particularly within the Republican Party. They might now think that if the Republican Party is to have any chance in 2028, this war should end now.

So there is a sense that there is more momentum for diplomacy after the election than there was before. In fact, when U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance wrote to Iran’s leadership, everybody said, “That’s exactly what we thought the consequence would be of the drubbing the administration got.”

But Trump is unpredictable. He has his own view of what’s in his interest as well as what’s in America’s interest. He may go back to a conflict with Iran, so we’re not out of the woods. But the Iranians know that the economic cost of the war is having a tangible political impact on America, as well as France and Germany, even if it is not being reflected in the stock market on a daily basis. Ultimately, these countries are likely to decide at some point that this war is not worth the political blowback it’s causing.

RA: I was going to come to how Tehran is gaming out the post-midterms scenario. You make the point that Iran may sense that it is getting some of what it wants—it is imposing costs. The flip side of that could be that the costs have been imposed, and Trump has now seen the worst of it. He seems to be doubling down. With nothing to lose, he may now be impervious to electoral pressure, meaning the Iranians may not have much leverage with him.

VN: That’s very true. This is a gamble. It doesn’t mean that because the elections happened, the Iranians can declare victory and say, “OK, we humiliated President Trump, now let’s sit down and talk.” Yes, they had a big win in terms of the midterm results, but now they have to deal with Trump.

I think the way they see it is that the decision to go to war is still uncomfortable for President Trump. It’s true that he’s not looking at another election, but the Republicans are looking at 2028. And ultimately, the longer this war goes on, the more the pressure it brings about, particularly if the next phase of the war is more destructive. The Iranians are broadcasting that loud and clear as a form of deterrence. They’re saying they’re going to wreak havoc on the region and the global economy, no holds barred.

If that’s the case, then President Trump still has to think about his legacy. He has to think about the future of the Republican Party. And he has to consider that if he enters into a wider war with Iran and he still isn’t able to defeat it, the scale of his failure will be even greater than it is now.

Until now, the most important factor on the American side hasn’t been some kind of a calculated, rational, technocratic national interest. It has been the psychology of one man from the get-go of deciding to go to war, to Trump’s various permutations.

Nobody—whether it’s the United States’ allies or Iran or China—can say with any certainty how Trump might actually act. So I think the Iranians are taking this one step at a time, reading the tea leaves and applying pressure where they can with Congress—particularly the new Congress coming in—to see whether they can avoid a big war and get a settlement that would benefit them.

The Iran war is most likely to end not with a bang, but with a whimper.

The U.S. military faces “profound” long-term readiness concerns, one expert said.

RA: Let me put something to you that I’ve been hearing from Trump administration officials I’ve spoken to on background. They say that Iran’s hand has significantly weakened over the last few months. The reasons they point to are that the amount of Gulf crude going through Hormuz has actually dropped significantly, from 83 percent to 60 percent, because Gulf states are using pipelines more and relying less and less on Hormuz. The rial is down to 1.7 million to the dollar. Recent U.S. sanctions have targeted the regime explicitly, as opposed to targeting the Iranian people. They’ve shut down parts of Mahan Air, which the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) relied on, as well as a United Arab Emirates branch of Banque Misr, which served the IRGC.

There’s a sense in the White House—or at least, it’s their narrative—that these measures must really hurt the people in power in Iran, and at some point the pain will be so great that the regime will be forced to make different choices. Do you buy any of that?

VN: No, I don’t. This is largely self-serving, anecdotal rhetoric, which has gotten us to this point. I don’t see a sign that the degree of pressure that this administration thinks it is imposing on Iran is actually being felt there and changing attitudes. When Iran sent its diplomats to the United Nations, the proposal they presented to the United States was an expansion of the Islamabad-brokered MOU, changed in ways that benefited Iran more. The Iranians referred to it as “Islamabad-Plus.”

Let’s also consider the fact that when Mecca defense alliance members and Pakistan’s army chief went to Tehran in October, Iran’s response to Vice President Vance’s letter was that Iran was sticking to its demands that Washington must fulfill all seven preconditions to resuming talks and reopening Hormuz. The United States’ pressure tactics are not working.

This administration doesn’t want to go to war or make a deal with Iran. They need to sell this “do nothing” strategy as a viable, working strategy, so they’re repackaging the blockade and saying it’s going to work this time. Two months ago, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was promising that there would be a run on the Iranian banks. That hasn’t happened. So I’m suspicious of these kinds of claims.

Iran is under a tremendous amount of pressure, but I don’t think the administration has a gauge of where the tipping point for them is. So it’s throwing a Hail Mary pass with more sanctions, hoping that they will do the trick, and then we get a lot of rhetoric trying to sell the current holding pattern as an actual strategy.

RA: So in response to everything you’re saying, Tehran’s strategy might be to wait out the next two years.

VN: If they have to, yes. Although, I don’t know. Iran’s supreme leader said in his last major statement that this “no war, no peace” situation is not sustainable or acceptable to Iran.

When and how will Iran disturb this status quo? We don’t know. We can already see that they are escalating pressure on shipping. In the past week, more of the shuttle ships that the UAE has been using to ferry oil from inside tankers in the Persian Gulf to tankers in the Arabian Sea are being hit. The number of mines installed in the Strait of Hormuz has also increased. More oil might go out on some days than others. So the Iranians are making the situation more unstable.

There is a difference between the way the West and Iran look at this situation. The U.S. and many observers think a move from Iran has to be really big to be meaningful. For instance, if they can’t bring the cost of oil to $200 a barrel, then they’ve lost.

But the Iranians actually do not look at things this way. Very early in the war, they understood that the price of oil is not 100 percent in their hands. What they believe works is sustained instability in the energy market. The costs of oil, natural gas, jet fuel, diesel, fertilizer, and so on are far above where they were. None of these measures by themselves will break the global economy, but if you put them together for long enough, they’re very effective.

Iran sees the result of the U.S. elections as proof of this concept. Their strategy is to boil the frog gradually. I think sometimes in the West we look at this and say, “Well, the frog is just sitting there and nothing’s happening to it, so the Iranians have failed.” So I think this is a race between whether Iran runs out of gas first or its strategy works.

Right now, I detect a certain degree of confidence from Iran. I don’t see the trepidation that observers on the outside are assuming the regime has. So either Iran has a good poker face, or it’s seeing the picture very differently than Washington does.

RA: It’s fascinating, and it’s always so hard to know whom or what to believe.

I’m curious what you make of these reports that claim to be from U.S. intelligence suggesting that Iran is developing plans to detonate a dirty bomb in Washington, D.C. These are just reports. Tehran, of course, denies that any of this is true, but I’m curious how you are reading that story and reacting to it.

VN: We’re in the midst of a war, and there’s a lot of disinformation. There are people in the United States or in Israel who want the United States to go back to war with Iran, to “finish the job,” so to speak. After the elections, which made it very obvious that the American public is firmly opposed to this war, you could only change that public opinion in favor of war if there was this kind of threat against America, or if you could posit that Iran is a deranged and crazy regime that must be taken out. So it could possibly be disinformation.

On the other hand, Iranians have also been taking certain actions as a form of deterrence. I don’t know whether Iran actually launched a cyberattack on refrigerators in France—French intelligence claims they did, though the Iranians deny it—but it’s a message. “If you attack us, then we have cyber capabilities that could paralyze your cities.”

It’s a kind of deterrence tactic. It’s a similar case with Iran issuing warnings to air bases in the U.K., which ultimately got the United States to move its airplanes from two different air bases. Some of this benefits Iran, because it’s a way of saying, “Do not assume that the next war would be cost-free. Our reach is much farther than you assume.”

It also suggests to me that Iran doesn’t want to go back to war. Rather, its game plan is to try to get the United States to come to the table to accept their terms.

Ravi Agrawal is the editor in chief of Foreign Policy. X: @RaviReports

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: How can one do Tax-loss harvesting on index funds? Large 100%+ capital gains, not-married, no property or loan

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Can we implement this personally. Does it have to be individual stocks. How can we do manually using just TLH pairs like VTI, VOO etc. Would options like puts and calls be used for long and short positions ?
I am a big believer in DIY. However....

It is complex. One needs a firm grasp of the tax code and portfolio theory. The portfolio theory relies heavily on upper level statistics and liner algebra. As a side bar you are going to need access to quality data.

It requires discipline. Frequent monitoring (at least weekly) and rebalancing/harvesting (at least every 6 weeks). As such, it requires time. And time is money.

In theory it is safe. Theory tends to unravel during times of crisis. So you need a game plan and even more discipline to act during times of crisis.

You want pairs with high Beta and low correlations. This argues for individual stocks. Options are in some way better. But that means you need to know option theory. So another level of complexity. And trading options is more expensive. Plus they tend to be more lumpy. So while the power is higher for a given dollar invested, it is also less precise for smaller portfolios.

Now, would I feel comfortable doing this? eh. I have done this professionally, so I am familiar with the tools and concepts. In my younger days yes. I do love a good spreadsheet. Today - well, I would rather spend time with my kid.

1Geometric Average: This weighted calculation uses each portfolio holding's market cap in a way designed to not skew the effect of very large or small holdings; instead, it aims to better identify the portfolio's center, which Royce believes offers a more accurate measure of average market cap than a simple mean or median.

2Harmonic Average: This weighted calculation evaluates a portfolio as if it were a single stock and measures it overall. It compares the total market value of the portfolio to the portfolio's share in the earnings of its underlying stocks.

The Price-to-Book, or P/B, Ratio is calculated by dividing a company's share price by its book value per share.

Net leverage is the percentage, in excess of 100 %, of the total value of equity type investments, divided by net assets.

New Homes Offer More Affordability for Buyers: Report

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With affordability remaining an issue at large in housing, new homes continue to represent a tempting alternative in a challenging market.

The latest data from the National Association of Home Builders (NAHB) found that affordability for new homes actually improved more than that of existing homes in Q3, focused on both home prices and rates.

The Q3 NAHB/Wells Fargo Cost of Housing Index (CHI) found that families earning the national median income of $106,800 needed 33% of their income to buy a median-priced new home, compared to 36% of their income for a median-priced existing home.

Low-income families (those who earn only 50% of the national median income) need 66% of their income to buy a new home, compared to 73% for an existing home.

NAHB Chairman Bill Owens noted that this was the “widest affordability advantage (for new homes) since the CHI began,” as builders “are working hard to offset rising costs.”

The report also noted that this is due in part to the difference in price between new homes ($400,400 median price) and existing homes ($416,700 median price), called out as the widest national median price gap since the CHI began.

The other attribution for the new home “affordability advantage” is the incentives and deals offered by builders.

Owens called out NAHB’s recent data on builder confidence, which saw 43% of builders offer price cuts in October—the highest share since 2020. Additionally, 65% of builders offered sales incentives.

A recent Realtor.com® report also found that many buyers purchasing new homes are seeing lower mortgage rates due to builders offering buy downs or other deals with mortgage lenders. The average starting mortgage rate of a new-home buyer was 5.01% in Q3, compared with 6.15% for existing-home buyers.

While new homes are offering better affordability than existing homes, overall housing affordability is still very much challenged.

For both new and existing homes, families earning the national median income remain fairly cost-burdened (paying more than 30% of their income for housing) or severely cost-burdened (paying more than 50% of their income for housing).

Of the 175 metro areas tracked in the report, 87 are cost-burdened by housing, and eight are severely cost-burdened. Leading the way among the eight severely cost-burdened markets is San Jose, California, where families use 79% of their income on housing.

Owens outlined that in order to move housing affordability in the right direction, “policymakers must do more to reduce red tape, address labor shortages and fix supply chains so builders can expand the nation’s housing supply.”

“As builders contend with rising construction costs, tighter lending standards and persistent labor shortages, policymakers should remove regulatory obstacles, enact permitting reform and create a stronger business environment that enables builders to produce more attainable, affordable homes,” added NAHB Chief Economist Robert Dietz.

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.

The Beatles Celebrate Liverpool in New Video for ‘In My Life’

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Beatles celebrate their hometown of Liverpool in the new video for the Fab Four classic “In My Life.”

The visual, starring Adolescence’s Owen Cooper, follows both the arrival of the Rubber Soul reissue — where “In My Life” features — as well as the recent announcement that The Beatles Story immersive experience is moving into bigger digs in Liverpool, where the band was born.

The nostalgic video, co-directed by Jessica Swale and Irina Alexiu, follows three generations in a musical family living in Liverpool, and how the gift of music is passed down and history repeats itself in surprising ways, with the British city serving as the backdrop for all the action, playing up the “places I’ll remember.”

Eagle eyed Beatles fans might spot some Easter Eggs over the course of the “In My Life” video, like Cooper and friends reenacting the iconic Abbey Road walk in front of Liverpool’s Woolton Picture House, or a nod to George Harrison’s tumble in A Hard Day’s Night.

Earlier this month, Apple Corps. announced plans to relocate The Beatles Story from Albert Dock to the previous site of St Johns Market, located just across from the Liverpool Lime Street station. “I love Liverpool. It’s part of who I am, it’s part of who The Beatles are. If you’d told us this was going to happen when we were hanging out and getting started we’d never have believed it,” Paul McCartney said in a statement.

“The extraordinary plans the Apple Corps team have shown me for The Beatles Story take me right back to that journey, from Liverpool to the world and back home again. It couldn’t have happened without Liverpool and I can’t wait for people to be able to step into our story and share the experience.”

GTA 6: How to Steal the Red Phoenix

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. With decades of experience in gaming and a full tour as a Gaming Journalist, Michael is ready to step up to the plate. From AAA to the smallest passion projects, his motto is that every piece of ar

Grand Theft Auto 6 keeps the actual mandatory crimes very grounded during its opening hours, far from the previous series openings of massive police shootouts and gang wars bursting at the seams. One of the very first jobs Jason is tasked with sounds fairly simple, but is shockingly involved.

Should you sell pure product or Tyrone’s cut batch in GTA 6? Compare the outcomes, risks, and rewards of each Dealer’s Choice option.

To secure some product left behind by an arrested junkie, Brian tasks Jason with securing a red Phoenix, a sleazy sports car with too much weed in the trunk to just walk away with. Getting it out of the police impound is easier said than done, so let’s go over it step by step.

Before you can yoink the car, you’ll need to start its mission, ‘Unwanted Attention’, by meeting with Cal at one of his fishing spots in Corsair Key. Mark his location via your text messages and drive to the northeast down the highway. These roads are tight, so be careful when trying to pull past other drivers.

Once you’ve snagged Cal, he’ll explain the job, marking the police impound where the Phoenix is currently being held. Once you arrive, you can choose between charging straight in with your newly acquired gear, or sneaking around the side through a hole in the barbed wire fence surrounding the other walls.

Stealthing through the impound is much simpler, with very few guards on patrol. If you’re looking to keep your Criminal Profile at a Professional level, hug the left side of the impound’s outer wall until you see an opening in the barbed wire, giving you an opportunity to hop in.

Now that you’re inside the impound itself, you’ll need to sneak past the two patrolling guards. One is wandering around the gate itself, while the other is deeper inside, near the red Phoenix. You’ll be prompted to use the Waink It app to help you identify the ride, but it always spawns in the northeast end of the impound, still hooked up to a winch.

The guards’ patrol patterns don’t overlap, so feel free to wander past or choke them out at your own discretion. Remember, nonlethal takedowns are easy ways to pivot your Criminal Profile towards Professional, or you can finish them off if you’re feeling a bit crueler.

Once you find the red Phoenix, don’t hop in immediately. Instead, enter the building behind it to find the guard manning the gate itself. Silently take him down, then hit a button on the wall to open the gate. Once the gate is open, lower the winch the Phoenix is attached to and hotwire it.

Now it’s time to bail! Drive straight through the open gate, and you’ll become Wanted, but since all the local cops are either unconscious or checking on their unresponsive allies, you’ve got a window before they arrive. Stick to backstreets or off-road routes to lose the cops quick.

Once you’re all clear, head back over to Hamlet and visit the marked Spray N’ Pray shop to change the car’s paint job. Any will do the trick, even if losing the Phoenix decal is a shame. Once you do, you’ll be able to drop it off at Brian’s Garage for your reward, just enough to buy a new outfit to get off the radar completely.

Plan for Long-Term Care in Your 50s – Your Savings Decide How You’ll Pay

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Medicare doesn’t pay for long-term care, so in your 50s, find out what care costs where you live while your health still qualifies you for coverage. Then choose how you’d pay by what you’ve saved: your own money if you’re wealthy, Medicaid if your assets are modest, and long-term care insurance if you’re in between.

Almost 70% of people turning 65 will need long-term care, meaning help with bathing, dressing, and other daily tasks, according to the Administration for Community Living. One in five will need it for more than five years.

Your age and health set what insurance costs and whether you can buy it. Most individual long-term care policies require a health review, so a diagnosis in your 60s can raise your price or rule you out.

The American Association for Long-Term Care Insurance’s 2026 price index shows a couple, both 55, paying about $5,000 a year combined, on average. That buys each of them a policy whose care benefits start at $165,000 and grow 3% a year. At 65, the same coverage averages about $7,000 a year.

Start by looking up typical prices in your area with CareScout’s cost of care calculator. Nationally, CareScout’s 2025 survey puts an in-home caregiver 44 hours a week at $80,080 a year, assisted living at $74,400, and a private nursing home room at $129,575.

Talk with your family, too, about who would give care and whether you’d stay in your own home or move to a facility. Ask your parents the same questions about their own care.

Get quotes for both kinds from an independent agent who sells several insurers’ policies. Identical coverage for an Illinois couple, both 60, ranged from $4,591 to $7,173 a year across five insurers in the same price index.

The creative and technical team behind Christopher Nolan’s “The Odyssey,” along with Patricia and the late David Keighley, will be honored at the Hollywood Professional Association Awards on Dec. 3.

“The Odyssey” creative team will receive the HPA Judges Award for Creativity and Innovation. The award recognizes companies and individuals who have made contributions to creative storytelling and technical innovation. It is not awarded annually but reserved for groundbreaking work.

Producer Emma Thomas will accept the award on behalf of the team, led by Nolan and including cinematographer Hoyte van Hoytema, editor Jennifer Lame, production designer Ruth De Jong, costume designer Ellen Mirojnick, composer Ludwig Göransson, visual effects supervisor Andrew Jackson, special effects supervisor Scott R. For more than 50 years, the Keighleys have worked on post-production for over 500 large-format documentary and feature films. They created and oversaw the development of the next-generation Imax Film Camera used to shoot “The Odyssey.”

The HPA Awards will be held Dec. 3 at 6 p.m. at the ASC Clubhouse in Hollywood.

‘The Eyes of Ghana’ Reaches 1 Million Views on YouTube in One Day

Ben Proudfoot’s acclaimed documentary “The Eyes of Ghana” has reached 1 million views on IShowSpeed’s YouTube channel in a single day.

The Breakwater Studios, Higher Ground and IShowSpeed production was released on YouTube on Tuesday following a livestream announcement the previous day between filmmakers Ben Proudfoot and Nana Adwoa Frimpong, Ethan Lewis from Higher Ground, Yaw Amponsah Apenteng, subject Chris Hesse, President Barack Obama and IShowSpeed.

The documentary follows Ghanaian cameraman Chris Hesse’s mission to rescue a hidden film archive documenting the birth of African independence. It is produced by IShowSpeed under his name Darren Watkin, directed by Ben Proudfoot and executive produced by President Barack Obama and Michelle Obama.

“Ghana and Africa mean so much to me,” IShowSpeed said in a statement. “Chris Hesse’s story matters and we’re not waiting on anybody to tell us which stories matter. We’re bringing this film to the world, for free. Chris Hesse spent his life protecting this important history. He deserves this moment. Ghana deserves this moment.”

Indie Distribution Exec Kyle Greenberg Exiting Utopia To Launch New Consultancy, Greenhouse

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Kyle Greenberg, longtime head of marketing and distribution at Utopia, is departing the company to launch a consulting startup called Greenhouse.

His new venture plans to develop highly curated, hands-on release strategies for filmmakers, artists, labels and brands to support their own efforts in self-distribution, something an increasing number of producers are turning to given challenges in the current indie film landscape.

Utopia was co-founded by Robert Schwartzman and Cole Harper in 2018. Greenberg helped launch the indie distribution, production and sales company, and its service-based Circle Collective division and has been a key player in growing both, overseeing creative, publicity and marketing, theatrical, home video, physical media and streaming for over 100 films. Related Stories News Utopia's Circle Collective Takes North American To 'Honeyjoon' Acquisitions Utopia's Circle Collective Acquires Greg Vrotsos' Silver Lake-Set 'Situations'

He championed breakout and award-winning features from first-time and auteur filmmakers like Emma Seligman (Shiva Baby), Jane Schoenbrun (We’re All Going To The World’s Fair), Kristoffer Borgli (Sick Of Myself), Sean Price Williams (The Sweet East), Amalia Ulman (El Planeta), Alex Ross Perry (Pavements), Gaspar Noe (Vortex), Anton Corbijn (Squaring The Circle, The Story of Hipgnosis), Errol Morris (American Dharma), Ali Abbasi (Holy Spider), the Ross Brothers (Bloody Nose, Empty Pockets) and others. He worked with Francis Ford Coppola to market and book Megalopolis, and with Mike Figgis on Megadoc. Watch on Deadline

His rollouts often merged non-traditional marketing and distribution, including bringing films to non-theatrical spaces with expansive elements like DJ sets, musical performances, art shows, pool parties and movie-date meetups, with a traditional, platformed theatrical or event strategy to build a footprint for independent and arthouse feature films across the U.S., U.K. and Canada.

“While so many people in the industry are lamenting a lack of reliable distribution methods, Greenhouse will be an extension of the ethos that has served as the foundation of my career — growth through disruption,” he said.

Greenberg is based in LA. He’ll be with Utopia through year end.

He also plans to scale up his own production label Stupid Co, created in 2024, alongside Greenhouse. Founded as a showcase for original work produced by Greenberg and partner Anna Maguire, Stupid Co expanded to events and experiences Cannes Dogs, Off-Viff and info labs Stupid Chats. It will release Greenberg and Maguire’s co-directed feature Galicia! in 2027. The hybrid documentary-eco horror comedy, shot on location at a winery in Spain, premiered at The Downtown Festival in NYC last month. The couple’s short films Hi! You Are Currently Being Recorded (2024) and French Lessons (2025) played about 70 festivals including Fantasia and SXSW Sydney.

In HelloNation, Business Performance Expert Martin Rowan of Naperville, IL, Explains How SAP Execution Impacts Operational Stability

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

NAPERVILLE, Ill., Nov. 19, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- What happens when SAP stops being used merely to explain results and starts being used to drive them? That question is addressed in a HelloNation article featuring insights from Martin Rowan, Managing Partner at Reveal USA, Inc. in Naperville, IL. The article explores how organizations can strengthen operational stability by moving beyond using SAP as a simple reporting layer and fully leveraging it for both planning and execution.

The article notes that many businesses still treat SAP as a reporting layer, pulling data into spreadsheets to make decisions. This practice, while common, often leads to fragmented workflows and reduced accountability. As highlighted in the HelloNation piece, when planning and execution occur outside SAP, teams face longer decision-making delays and greater inconsistencies across departments. This increase in decision latency can negatively affect overall business performance, leaving organizations to react to problems rather than prevent them.

According to the article, SAP execution becomes far more effective when it is positioned at the center of both planning and operational processes. Rowan explains that teams relying on spreadsheets miss out on version control, automated checks, and system-wide integration. These gaps lead to avoidable errors, duplicate work, and misaligned strategies. The article underscores that restoring execution in SAP enhances control, speeds decision-making, and enables proactive management of business operations.

The HelloNation article emphasizes that a fully integrated SAP environment improves both decision speed and outcome reliability. When SAP execution is prioritized, teams can access real-time information that empowers them to act faster and with more confidence. This operational visibility also increases accountability, as everyone works from the same data source. Business performance becomes easier to manage because processes are consistent, and service levels stabilize across the board.

Another important takeaway from the article is the financial benefit of aligning planning and execution within SAP. Companies can free up working capital by streamlining procurement and inventory strategies. When execution decisions are based on current SAP data, rather than disconnected spreadsheet estimates, unnecessary expenditures are reduced. SAP becomes not only a planning tool but a driver of financial efficiency and operational effectiveness.

As the article explains, the shift from using SAP as a reporting layer to a full planning and execution system requires more than just technology. It demands a cultural shift within organizations. Teams must be willing to trust SAP to manage operational tasks in real time. With this shift, companies reduce decision latency, improve transparency, and make faster, more informed decisions. This kind of commitment leads to measurable gains in business performance and long-term stability.

The HelloNation feature also highlights that employees benefit from this transformation. By eliminating the need to reconcile conflicting spreadsheets, teams can focus on tasks that add value to the business. With SAP execution in place, operations become more predictable and efficient, improving the experience for both employees and customers alike.

The article titled "When SAP Stops Explaining Results and Starts Delivering Them" features insights from Martin Rowan, Business Performance Expert in Naperville, IL, in HelloNation.

NFL predictions: Our picks for Giants-Jaguars, the full Week 11 slate

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The New York Giants face major test this week when they face the Jacksonville Jaguars. It is also a game that will provide a huge swing in the Giants' expected chances to make the playoffs. Will the Giants upset Jacksonville? See what our Big Blue View staff members think about that game and the rest of the Week 11 action in this week's staff picks.

I think there's a path to a win for the Giants, if they can find it. The Giants are one of the very best teams in the NFL at forcing drives to end in punts. At the same time, the Jaguars will be without their starting center and the Giants are one of the best teams in the League at creating quick pressure. That's important because Trevor Lawrence has been one of the quarterbacks most affected by pressure in the NFL.

At the same time, the Giants have rediscovered the explosive pass with J.J. McCarthy, which has been the weakness of the Jags' defense.

If the Giants can score touchdowns while disrupting Lawrence, they've got the chance to come away with the upset. We've seen the Giants rebound after bad games, as well as play much better than anticipated when they're underdogs. Maybe both will happen here.

The flip side of the coin is simpler and scarier to me: The Giants have one of the worst red zone defenses in the NFL, and the Jaguars have one of the best red zone offenses in the league. If the Giants can't stop them between the 20s, this game could get ugly fast.

Jacksonville is one of the hottest teams in the league with a young and explosive offense that can run the football. Their defense is aggressive but doesn't NEED to bring the blitz — they blitz at about a league-average rate, but Anthony Campanile does an excellent job utilizing simulated pressure to harass opposing quarterbacks. Jacksonville is third in offensive EPA, 4th in defensive EPA allowed, and 1st in Net EPA — they're a juggernaut based on their performance and the advanced analytics.

Still, that doesn't mean the Giants don't have a chance — they just must play efficient football and avoid the catastrophic mistakes that have robbed them of wins. It's possible, but against the most efficient team in the NFL, it will be difficult. John Harbaugh will have the Giants ready and prepared for the game, and I believe it will be more exciting and competitive. Jacksonville is concluding their third straight road game, which could give the Giants a slight advantage at home, but I ultimately believe the better team finds a way to win.

It took 11 weeks, but for the first time this season, the Giants will face a team with a winning record when the 7-2 Jaguars come to town on Sunday. Not only is Jacksonville running away with the AFC South (they lead the division by four games), but they're steamrolling the competition. They've won their last three games by a combined 75 points. This is easily Big Blue's toughest test of the season, so far.

Jacksonville is very good on both sides of the ball. They're fourth in offensive DVOA, third in defensive DVOA, and third in overall DVOA. They also have an excellent field goal kicker with crazy range. If you look at both rosters, it's hard to find clear advantages for the Giants at any position group outside of tight end, and even there, Jacksonville has good players.

Can the Giants win this game? Yes. They're at home, they're getting an extra three days of rest, and as we saw when they played Houston, they aren't scared and can surprise as a big underdog. They'll need to dial it up on defense and pressure Trevor Lawrence into the kinds of mistakes he used to make before Liam Coen got there. They also might have a little extra motivation after letting last week's game against the Commanders slip away at the end.

I won't be surprised if John Harbaugh's team hangs in there and keeps it as a one-score game most of the way. But in the end, until the Giants show me that they can beat a really good team, I can't pick them to do so.

You can talk yourself into believing that the Giants can beat Jacksonville this Sunday. They needed a miraculous last-second 57-yard field goal to beat the Eagles team (with Jalen Hurts) that the Giants defeated (without Hurts). They lost to the Texans team that the Giants beat the following week. The woeful Titans were within a TD against them last week with 5 minutes left. So indeed, it could be a competitive game. Unfortunately, the Jags have also won games by 24, 29, 35, and 23 points this season. They have scored 31 or more in each of their past four games, and over 40 in two of those. The Giants have only scored more than 30 points once, and only then because of a 99-yard pick-6 on the last play of the game.

Trevor Lawrence isn't necessarily playing any better this year than in the past. He's a QB that simultaneously makes great passes and puts the ball up for grabs, yet unlike in years past, he's cut down on the INTs while getting the TDs. Part of that may be due to 5'10" wide receiver Parker Washington (a 2023 6th-round pick), who had a breakout 2025 and is on pace for 1,000 yards. Running back Bhayshul Tuten, a 4th round 2025 pick, isn't quite in the Jahmyr Gibbs – Kenneth Walker class but he's not far below and is a missed tackle machine. A lot of the credit has to go to head coach Liam Coen, who also calls the plays and turned this team around as soon as he was hired. Lawrence, like most QBs, has trouble with pressure, but he is not too fazed by the blitz. Finding a way to disrupt his rhythm is a key to any chance the Giants may have.

On defense, the Jaguars are strong at linebacker with fourth-round 2023 pick Ventrell Miller, one of the league's best (note how many mid-round picks the Jags have hit on), along with Foyesade Oluokun. They also put on a decent pass rush with Josh Hines-Allen, Travon Walker, and Dennis Gardeck. In the secondary, Travis Hunter is playing more like a No. 2 pick, having mostly given up his evening gig as a receiver.

Are the Giants a serious playoff contender? This is the type of game they can use to make a statement against a team that is in the Super Bowl conversation. I think they'll put up a fight. I also don't think they're there yet.

I want to convince myself to pick the Giants to defeat the Jaguars. I really do. I just can't. The Jaguars are a team with justifiable Super Bowl ambitions. They are a well-rounded team with a high-powered offense, a very good defense, and one of the best field-goal kickers in the business in Cam Little.

The Giants are a good team. They are resilient. They will give the Jaguars their best shot. Maybe, just maybe, it will be good enough to pull off an upset that would go a long way toward ensuring that the Giants make the playoffs.

2 Growth Stocks to Buy in November Without Any Hesitation

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Vertex and Regeneron started 2026 slowly, but blockbuster results and deep pipelines have both stocks climbing again.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:43PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vertex Pharmaceuticals ›

Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX -2.00%) and Regeneron (REGN -0.88%) did not have a strong start to the year -- both biotech giants were in decline for much of the first half of 2026. However, they have rebounded, and as of this writing, both have delivered above-average returns this year. They haven't peaked yet: Vertex Pharmaceuticals and Regeneron arguably remain excellent stocks to buy this month and hold onto over the medium term. Let me explain.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals could soon receive approval for a brand-new product, povetacicept. This investigational medicine for IgA nephropathy (a kidney disease) performed well in a Phase 3 study earlier this year, and regulators in the U.S. are expected to decide whether to approve it or not by the end of November. Povetacicept would be a nice addition to Vertex's lineup, which is still dominated by its cystic fibrosis (CF) medicines.

The drugmaker derives most of its revenue from CF therapies, an area where it is the undisputed leader. In the third quarter, Vertex's total revenue increased by 12% year over year to $3.44 billion. Vertex is unlikely to face stiff competition, generic or otherwise, within its core CF market anytime soon. But newer medicines, like povetacicept, will help expand and diversify its lineup.

Povetacicept could be the first of several drugs across kidney diseases for which Vertex earns approval. The company's pipeline also includes inaxaplin, an investigational medicine for APOL1-mediated kidney disease that is currently undergoing late-stage studies. Then there is VX-407, which is being developed to treat autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease; VX-407 is in Phase 2 studies. Between them, these three drugs could eventually generate over $10 billion in peak annual sales, according to some estimates, which isn't that far off from the annual revenue it currently makes from its CF franchise.

Further, Vertex recently expanded its lineup and pipeline through the $10 billion acquisition of Crinetics Pharmaceuticals. The biotech estimated that the products it acquired through this buyout could eventually generate more than $5 billion in peak annual sales. And we haven't considered the company's newer launches, such as Casgevy, a gene-editing medicine for two rare blood diseases, and Journavx, a therapy for acute pain. Vertex boasts several other pipeline candidates, too. Vertex's prospects look very attractive as it continues posting excellent results while making meaningful clinical and regulatory progress.

Regeneron's financial results have smashed expectations this year, powered by a strong performance from its biggest growth driver, Dupixent, a medicine for eczema that it co-markets with Sanofi (SNY -2.44%). In the third quarter, Regeneron's revenue increased by 36% year over year to $5.1 billion. That was the fastest quarterly year-over-year growth rate the biotech company has recorded in years.

REGN Revenue (Quarterly YoY Growth) data by YCharts

Dupixent's total sales (as reported by Sanofi) were $6.5 billion, up 35% from the year-ago period. Regeneron is achieving impressive financial results despite continued pressure on its Eylea franchise. This medicine for several eye-related disorders has been facing stiff competition, biosimilar and otherwise.

But a newer, higher-dose (HD) formulation of Eylea with a more convenient dosing schedule is helping to smooth out the losses. In the third quarter, U.S. sales of Eylea HD increased 62% year over year to $699 million, although total Eylea and Eylea HD combined U.S. sales declined by 1% year over year to $1.1 billion.

Still, overall, Regeneron is performing very well, and the company could maintain solid momentum as the impact of the older version of Eylea on its financial results fades. Meanwhile, Regeneron boasts an attractive pipeline across oncology, weight loss, fatty liver disease, and other areas. The company should have a much stronger approved lineup within the next few years.

And while Dupixent could lose patent exclusivity in the early 2030s, Regeneron and Sanofi are planning to launch another formulation of the blockbuster drug that could grant it a more convenient dosing schedule and help it retain a decent market share.

That, combined with newer products it should launch by then, could allow Regeneron to continue posting solid financial results and deliver superior returns.
