Europe Spent €16.5 Million Proving Again That Hydrogen Burns in a Turbine

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hydrogen burns. Turbines spin. Their exhaust can make steam. Kawasaki demonstrated hydrogen-fired electricity and heat production at Kobe Port Island in 2018, before Europe funded the first HYFLEXPOWER programme. Six years later, Siemens Energy is still issuing announcements about hydrogen combustion at a single turbine in a French paper mill. European and British taxpayers have supplied roughly €16.5 million for the two research programmes, and the same installation has generated at least 78 identified press appearances. Neither the grants nor the publicity have produced a commercial reason to make hydrogen from electricity and burn it for industrial electricity and steam.

The latest Siemens Energy announcement, dated November 16, 2026, describes an SGT-400 turbine at Smurfit Westrock’s Saillat-sur-Vienne paper mill operating on pure hydrogen through startup, load changes and full-load cogeneration. The equipment can operate under those conditions. Hydrogen combustion and heat recovery were already established. Extending the operating range of one manufacturer’s turbine has not changed hydrogen’s production cost, the losses involved in making it or the available alternatives. No second commercial installation has emerged from the programme.

Siemens’ own March 2024 technical article supplied the most important numbers. The electrolyzer installed at the paper mill produces 16.4 kg of hydrogen per hour. The turbine consumes about 810 kg per hour when producing 10 MW of electricity. Onsite production covers 2.02% of hourly fuel demand. Nearly 50 times that electrolyzer capacity would be needed to supply the turbine continuously at the stated operating point. Later testing used hydrogen brought to the site.

The 2023 campaign accumulated about 90 hours of hydrogen and natural-gas blending tests, including just over four hours on pure hydrogen. Siemens identified limited hydrogen availability as the constraint. The 2026 announcement supplies no cumulative full-load hydrogen operating hours, delivered fuel price or routine cost of electricity and steam. These are the figures an industrial customer needs before buying equipment. Announcing another combustion test avoids the question of who will supply hundreds of kilograms of affordable hydrogen every hour for years.

My complete TFIE Strategy Briefing investigation examines the funding records, Siemens’ published operating figures, the energy balance and an audited chronology of 78 press appearances surrounding the same turbine.

At an illustrative 50 kWh of electricity per kilogram of hydrogen, manufacturing the turbine’s 810 kg of hourly fuel requires approximately 40.5 MW of electricity. The turbine returns about 10 MW of electricity plus usable exhaust heat. Compression, storage and delivery add equipment and expense. The mill needs electricity and process steam; converting electricity into gas and burning it to recover those services introduces losses and costs that direct electrification avoids.

France is an especially poor location for this detour. RTE’s 2025 electricity review reports that 95.2% of French generation was low-carbon, with average generation emissions of 19.6 gCO₂e/kWh. Direct grid power can supply the electrical load. Industrial heat pumps, mechanical vapour recompression, electric boilers and thermal storage can supply process heat and steam in combinations designed around the mill’s operating requirements. The programme should have compared these complete systems before committing public money to more hydrogen turbine retrofits.

A paper mill in Finland is already doing the industrial work. Delfort’s Tervakoski plant has operated a Turboden heat pump and mechanical vapour-recompression installation since January 2026. It supplies about 12 MW of process steam at temperatures up to 170°C, with a reported coefficient of performance around two. It delivers useful steam without a hydrogen-production plant, hydrogen deliveries or a hydrogen-fired turbine. The exact plant integration will vary, but the alternative is operating commercially while HyCoFlex continues issuing test announcements.

The funding was directed at the wrong problem. HYFLEXPOWER received €10.48 million in EU support. HyCoFlex received another €4.44 million. A Siemens presentation identifies approximately €1.56 million more in UK support, bringing the public contribution across the programmes to about €16.48 million. Both programmes were designed around making existing gas-turbine cogeneration equipment compatible with hydrogen. The grants paid for additional turbine operating conditions instead of establishing that hydrogen was a sensible way to provide the plant’s energy.

The programme’s economics were weak before the second award. In October 2023, Siemens presented a scenario requiring electricity below €35/MWh to bring hydrogen fuel costs into parity with natural gas under its 2022 cost assumptions. The HYFLEXPOWER final workshop later examined favourable operating cases with hydrogen substitution and electrolyzer utilization both above 90%. European reporting also identified renewable electricity below €40/MWh as a condition for favourable deployment economics. France’s 2025 wholesale electricity averaged €61.1/MWh. Its negative-price hours were occasional. They could not supply a plant operating more than 90% of the time. These conditions were set out before the research programme ended, yet funding continued for more combustion trials.

Siemens also has a clear commercial interest in preserving turbine markets. Its 2025 presentation explicitly described protecting existing assets and future gas-turbine investments in a decarbonizing world as a benefit of hydrogen compatibility. A turbine supplier is entitled to pursue its products with its own money. The purpose of public industrial-decarbonization funding is to reduce emissions and costs, not to protect combustion-equipment sales from direct-electric competition.

The TFIE media audit counted 19 press appearances in 2020, 13 in the 2022 installation and blending phase, 35 around the 2023 tests and award, four in 2024 and seven through November 16, 2026. Those 78 appearances trace back to a handful of announcements about one installation. They are not 78 independent engineering advances or 78 deployments. The headline count swelled while the operating fleet did not.

Public funders should require equipment-neutral comparisons of delivered industrial energy services before underwriting another hydrogen retrofit. The evidence needed is familiar: a year’s reliable electricity and steam, actual operating cost, lifecycle emissions, fuel contracts, and performance against direct-electric alternatives. HyCoFlex has supplied more hydrogen-combustion publicity without establishing those outcomes. The problem was not that Europe failed to make a turbine burn hydrogen. It was that public money kept flowing after the relevant energy and economic questions already had answers.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing investigation contains the audited funding and media evidence, the 2% onsite hydrogen-supply calculation and the direct-electric industrial steam comparison. TFIE Strategy provides evidence-based assessments of industrial energy investments and public programmes.

Going Up: Sharp Uptick in Rates After Four Weeks of Declines

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Estate tax,Model house with Percentage symbol icon on green background,Business investment and Property tax concept

Mortgage rates moved higher this week, but experts say even against the backdrop of potential Fed rate increases and record-low home sales, there are some advantages in the housing market for buyers who remain active.

The latest Freddie Mac Primary Mortgage Market Survey® released Thursday shows the average 30-year fixed-rate mortgage rate at 7.28%, up from 7.11% last week. The 30-year FRM is still up year-over-year from 6.26% last year. The 15-year FRM averaged 6.66%, up from last week’s 6.45%. A year ago at this time, the 15-year FRM averaged 5.54%.

At press time, the average rate was up to 7.46% Thursday afternoon, according to Mortgage News Daily.

Bright MLS Chief Economist Lisa Sturtevant broke down the numbers for buyers, explaining at current interest rates, and assuming a 10% down payment and average property tax and home insurance costs, the monthly payment on the median-priced home in the U.S. is $3,100. A homebuyer needs an income of over $130,000 to qualify to purchase the median priced home. The median household income in the U.S. is currently about $90,000.  Renters have lower incomes, with a median of about $60,000, she said.

“Last year at this time, when mortgage rates were 100 basis points lower, the median mortgage payment was $250 lower. The difference is enough to price some prospective homebuyers out of the market,” Sturtevant added. “There are other would-be homebuyers who could take on this higher rate but instead are looking ahead to 2027 hoping for more favorable conditions.”

She also said the desire for homeownership remains strong, particularly among young adults, according to recent surveys. “We should expect buyers to return to the market in the coming years; however, higher rates are forcing many young adults to delay, which means they are also delaying the wealth accumulation that comes through homeownership.”

Realtor.com Senior Economist Anthony Smith said hot inflation data and the persistence of the conflict in Iran is placing upward pressure on bond yields and borrowing costs.

“The October CPI report came in above expectations on both the headline and core measures, cementing market expectations for a December rate hike and giving the 10-year little room to fall.

“The debate has shifted from whether the Fed hikes in December to how aggressively it moves,” he added, noting that backdrop is tough for mortgage rates heading into what is already a seasonally slow stretch for the housing market.

“Pending home sales fell to a record low in October, and existing home sales hit their lowest level since 2010, as the surge in rates through mid-October took its toll on buyers and sellers alike. Builder confidence has also fallen to a 14-year low, and permit activity dropped to its lowest level since the early days of the pandemic, signaling that the supply pipeline will face continued pressure in the months ahead,” Smith said.

But for buyers who remain active, Smith noted conditions offer some real advantages.

“With fewer shoppers in the market, competition has thinned, sellers have shown more flexibility on price, and active inventory continues to rise even as new listings have pulled back. The challenge is that until inflation cools enough to put mortgage rates on a durable downward path, affordability will remain the dominant constraint on the market.”

The proposed fund structure brings together two things investors are obsessed with.

Mortgage Purchase and Refi Activity Hits New Low in Q3

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Data for the third quarter for 2026 continues to showcase the effects of high rates and elevated housing-related costs, as the latest data reveals that the quarterly slowdown was driven by declines in both home purchase and refinance lending.

ATTOM—the California-based property data and real estate analytics company—found that all mortgages and lending in housing fell in Q3 2026, in the latest U.S. Residential Property Mortgage Origination Report.

According to the report, a total of 1.29 million purchase and refinance mortgages secured by residential property (one in four units) originated in the third quarter, which is down 12% from Q2 2026 and down 15% compared to the same period last year.

“The increase in refinance activity we saw late last year and into early this year cooled in the third quarter, with refinancing declining more than purchase lending,” ATTOM CEO Rob Barber said. “Purchase loans also slipped to their lowest third-quarter level since 2012, as higher mortgage rates and elevated average purchase loan amounts continued to strain affordability for many buyers.”

Barber—alongside other economists—attribute the third quarter’s cooling housing activity to the sharp jump in mortgage rates. After the Federal Reserve’s rate hike in September, the average 30-year fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.40% in mid-October, and has slowly decreased but remains over 7%.

Purchase loans fell by 9% quarter-to-quarter, for a total of nearly 730,000. The report notes that this drop is the lowest third quarter since 2012. Annually, purchase loans were down 12%, although the average purchase loan increased by 3% to nearly $412,000.

Purchase lending was also down in Q3 2026, totaling $300.6 billion or down 10% from last quarter, and down 10% on a yearly basis.

While activity declined, purchase loans accounted for 56.5% of all purchase and refinance originations, which is up from the 54.5% in Q2 2026 and Q3 2025.

Looking locally, 73.6% of the analyzed metros experienced declines in purchase activity. Some cities with populations over 1 million did see quarterly increases:

But even densely-populated cities were not immune to the effect of elevated mortgage rates, with these metros experiencing the steepest quarterly drops:

Alongside purchase loans, refinance activity fell 16% quarter-to-quarter, totaling 561,290 loans. This marks the lowest quarterly total since Q2 2024, and is down 19% from the same period last year.

The share of refinance originations decreased slightly, from 45.5% in Q2 to 43.5% in Q3 2026. Although not a significant drop in shares, refinance lending declined more drastically than purchase activity by city, falling 89.4% on a quarterly basis.

The cities with the worst drops were mostly in the South and along the West Coast:

Breaking it down by government-backed activity, Federal Housing Administration (FHA) loans accounted for 15.7% of all purchase and refinance originations in the third quarter, down from the previous quarter’s 16.1% and last year’s 17.9%. VA-backed loans were also down, representing 6.9% of originations in Q3 after the previous quarter’s 8.2% and 7.2% the same period last year, or the lowest share since Q2 2024.

Again, the 2026 housing market is shaping up to have a muted end, as pending home sales and mortgage applications have been on a downward trend for the past few months.

The increase in housing construction may spell some hope for housing demand, although experts did express skepticism based on longer-term affordability challenges and economic uncertainty.

GTA 6’s Best-Kept Secret Is the Most Important Part of the Game

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The prologue of GTA 6 starts with a shock

Considering how much of Grand Theft Auto 6 leaked before its launch, you’d think that Rockstar’s latest wouldn’t have anything left to hide. I mean, we’ve really seen it all. But as players immediately discovered when starting their save file, Rockstar (kind of) managed to keep one secret — and it was a huge one.

[Ed. note: This article contains spoilers for the prologue of GTA 6.]

GTA 6 kicks off with a short prologue chapter that sets a few things in motion. First, we get to see the moment Jason and Lucia met, as they both got crossed trying to jack the same car. A rom-com montage ensues, but things take a turn fast. We fast-forward to Lucia sitting in a restaurant as she prepares to pull off a robbery across the street. After nervously stalling​​​​​​​, she works up the courage to stick the place up. Unfortunately, it doesn’t go well. The cops quickly intervene and arrest her.

The twist comes as she’s being put into the back of a cop car. A panicked Lucia begs the cops to take her back to the restaurant where she was holed up before the robbery, because she left something there. The cops discover something that the camera was carefully hiding earlier in the sequence: Lucia’s infant son, Tommy, who she left unattended.

As we soon learn, Tommy is Jason and Lucia’s son — unbeknownst to Jason, until Lucia tells him upon getting out of prison. Tommy now lives with a separate guardian after getting taken from Lucia’s custody; Jason and Lucia only get to see him through occasional photos that get texted to them. It turns out that Jason and Lucia’s whole motivation for making cash in GTA 6 is to fund a family savings account in hopes that they can reunite with their son and give him a normal life.

It’s a shocking revelation that Rockstar desperately wanted to keep hidden as long as possible. No pre-release trailers for the game so much as teased that there was a kid in the picture. When I spoke to Rockstar North co-head Rob Nelson just ahead of the game’s launch, he seemed relieved that the detail had mostly gone unspoiled, despite a cavalcade of leaks. (It actually did leak about a year ago, but it faded into a forgotten rumor in a sea of them by the time GTA 6 actually launched.) For Nelson, that initial shock is an important part of an experience that’s designed to challenge what you think you know about its antiheroes at every turn.

With Tommy comes a mess of complicated character dynamics and anxieties that you’ll see throughout GTA 6’s story. While Lucia seems like an impulsive loudmouth who doesn’t have a care in the world, it becomes increasingly clear that she’s harboring serious insecurity after having Tommy taken from her. She quietly worries that she doesn’t deserve to be a parent after leaving her kid to go pull off a stick-up. While Jason wasn’t part of that, he holds the same fear that someone like him isn’t responsible enough to be a father. Those feelings bleed out whenever the two receive a text with a photo of Tommy and are reminded of the kid who is growing up somewhere without them.

Tommy also adds some welcome tension into Jason and Lucia’s love story, considering that the romance can be largely free of friction otherwise (depending on how you engage with the romance system). At one point, the two broke into a spontaneous conversation about their family. Jason noted that Lucia’s mother doesn’t seem to like him very much, which prompted Lucia to reveal that she lied to her mom and told her that Jason didn’t want to be involved in Tommy’s life. Though that isn’t exactly true given that Jason didn’t know his son existed, Lucia then recalled a conversation where Jason told her he didn’t want to have kids. That exchange gives us context that explains why Lucia didn’t tell Jason about Tommy upfront. For Nelson, those little details help build a nuanced story of two reluctant parents trying to figure out how to adapt to an unexpected responsibility.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

And while stories about parenting might feel a little played out in a world where the “dad game” has become a tired trope, it’s refreshing to have one that has so much compassion for everyone involved. It’s sympathetic to Lucia’s circumstances, making a point in a flashback to show her struggling to live as a single mother in a world that is hostile to her specific needs. It is careful about letting Jason face some very human insecurities without casting him as a hothead who can only deal in tough love. GTA 6 tells us that two people can survive a bad situation if they’re driven by a love that provides stability in uncertain times.

As players are bound to experience for themselves, Tommy changes so much about the psychology of GTA, even from off-screen. Maybe he won’t make you think twice about robbing a bank, but hopefully you’ll feel like all that crime isn’t for nothing every time you send a few bucks into your savings account.

Rockstar’s magnum opus searches for love and catharsis in desperate times — and finds them

Dreaming of Buying a Home Abroad? These 10 Countries Make It Shockingly Simple

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you’ve always wanted to live out your expat fantasies, start here.

Over the last few years, many Americans have daydreamed about life abroad. Moving internationally can often feel like a pipe dream, but with the right planning, it’s possible. However, lengthy visa processes, daunting language barriers, and a difficult-to-navigate legal system can make the dream feel more like a nightmare.

Certain countries, like Australia, Canada, and New Zealand, have bans on foreign buyers. Others, like Thailand, Cuba, and Vietnam, don’t allow outsiders to own property at all. On the other end of the spectrum are countries actively trying to attract foreign buyers, offering enviable incentives like affordable home prices, a smoother customs process, and an easier-to-navigate work-visa approval process.

Choosing the right location can make moving abroad much less intimidating. Ahead, we’re breaking down the ten best countries for Americans looking to buy property abroad. Whether you’re dreaming of relocating or searching for your perfect vacation home, these countries make buying as easy as possible and offer gorgeous landscapes too.

If you’ve ever dreamed of living among medieval architecture and rolling mountain ranges, Georgia might be the key to unlocking your fantasy. A bridge between Eastern Europe and Western Asia, the transcontinental country borders Russia, Turkey, Armenia, and the Black Sea, offering both subtropical and high-altitude climates. Thankfully, the real estate landscape is much less complex, with few legal or bureaucratic hurdles for American buyers, no residency or visa required (for up to a year), and remarkably fast processing times. In fact, the only major restriction in the Georgia market is that foreigners cannot purchase or own agricultural land, a nod to the importance of the local, small-scale farming cultures peppered across the country’s regions.

Uruguay’s buying process could not be more straightforward. South America’s second-smallest sovereign country requires no citizenship, visa, or prior approvals. Under Uruguayan law, foreign buyers are given the same rights as citizens and enjoy the benefits of free capital flow. Locals are just as, if not more, tourist-friendly. The country is known for its hospitality, laid-back café culture, and stunning coastal landscapes.

Instead of retiring to Florida, consider this balmy, beautiful Caribbean country—just a four-hour direct flight from the New York area. Like everything else on the island, the real estate laws are relatively relaxed, making buying a house easy-breezy; and you can buy and own property without obtaining residency status or special permissions, as is the case in many other Caribbean islands. “Foreigners can own property outright in the Dominican Republic, with legal protections for foreign investors,” says Mauricio Umansky, CEO and founder of The Agency. One important consideration is financing: You can certainly get a mortgage in the DR, but it may not be quite as cushy as what you can get in the U.S.—so pay cash if possible.

Great news if you’ve been wanting a more provincial life: Americans face zero restrictions when buying homes in France. The open market allows for statesiders to buy houses, apartments, or plots of land in the city, countryside, and coastal towns in person or remotely without needing citizenship, residency, or even a local visa. However, a purchase does not grant residency, so if you’re hoping to make France your permanent home, you’ll want to apply for a separate long-stay visa and note the Schengen 90-out-of-180-day rule. While buying may be simple, the finances are anything but. Closing fees, including notary fees, typically add seven to ten percent to your purchase. Mortgage rates are also incredibly hard to secure for U.S.-based buyers, thanks to strict debt-to-income caps and FATCA compliance. To simplify the process, many buyers opt to put 20 percent to 40 percent down in cash.

Perhaps the most American-friendly market, Panama boasts a large English-speaking market, virtually no restrictions on foreign property ownership, and uses the U.S. dollar as legal tender in a vastly affordable market. The Central American nation also offers residency programs like the Friendly Nations Visa, clearing the way legally for residency by investment. In addition to an efficient legal system and reliable market, Panama boasts breathtaking beaches, lush rainforests, and exceptional wildlife.

If you’re hoping for a home in the coffee valleys of Colombia, you’ll need to hire a real estate lawyer before you start scouting for mountain chalets, coffee triangle treehouses, and rainforest retreats. The Caribbean country opts out of standardized agent licensing systems, making legal aid essential to verify title and freedom certificates. While citizenship isn’t required, you will need to secure a tourist visa and Colombian Tax ID to complete your purchase and register for property taxes.

The buying process in Greece is lengthy, though it is streamlined, with a clear checklist for American buyers to find success in the market. Hiring a local lawyer or notary is a great first step after scouting properties. Not only are they familiar with title checks and contracts, but they can also navigate the intricacies that come with buying in designated border areas or near military zones, which require special clearance for non–EU buyers. While the most popular areas for American buyers, like Athens and Crete, don’t come with heavy legal intricacies, buying any property in Greece will mean securing a Greek Tax Number—a small price to pay for cities with such rich histories and breathtaking seaside views.

The people in this popular Western European coastal country are famously friendly and inviting, and that extends to real estate. According to Samira Easton, a certified international property specialist with Premier Sotheby’s International Realty, Portugal offers a Golden Visa Program, which makes it simple to buy property and gain residency; clear real estate laws; and a well-established network of English-speaking real estate agents, lawyers, and notaries to help you during the process.

Belize is home to one of the most vibrant, tourist-friendly cultures in Central America, mixing Mayan, Hispanic, Asian, and Creole cultures in its dense rainforests and rolling low plains. Buying here is as easy as it can get, as outright ownership applies to foreigners, and a British-inspired legal system abolishes language barriers and makes contracts easier to understand. Even more, there is no capital gains tax in Belize, and property taxes trend low. The country also boasts vibrant marine life and is home to the world’s second-largest barrier reef.

Spain may have ended its investor visa program in 2025, but the real estate market is still remarkably easy for Americans to navigate. The country currently imposes no restrictions on foreign or nonresident property ownership, but there are a few things you’ll need to procure before you buy, like a Foreigner Identification Number, which is mandatory for taxes. Despite the minor hurdles, the market is booming both on the ground and online. “There are also numerous online resources and property portals available, so it’s easy to find listings and information,” Easton says. This is a boon for those who aren’t able to make the trip over to Europe to house shop in person.Note that buying property does not grant you residency or bypass the country’s 90-day Schengen limit. If you’re hoping to turn your Spanish holiday into your everyday, you’ll have to apply for a separate visa.

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

Perpetual futures are the engine room of crypto trading. Centralized exchanges processed $85.3 trillion in perps trading volume in 2025 alone, according to CoinGecko's State of Crypto Perpetuals Report 2026, and the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) describes perps as the dominant form of crypto derivatives trading.

They aren't only for crypto, either. Exchanges now offer perps on stocks, commodities and currencies, and Decrypt has become the first news publisher to offer perps trading, powered by Solana exchange Phoenix.

This guide explains what futures are, what makes perpetual futures different, how funding rates and liquidations work, and what to know before trading them.

To understand perpetual futures, it helps to start with ordinary futures.

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a set price on a set date in the future. Futures have been used for centuries: farmers used them to lock in prices for crops before harvest, and today they are traded on everything from oil and wheat to stock indexes and Bitcoin, on regulated exchanges such as CME.

Traders use futures for two main reasons. Hedgers use them to protect against price swings; an airline might buy oil futures to lock in fuel costs, for example. Speculators use them to bet on price movements without owning the underlying asset.

Traditional futures have an expiry date. When a contract expires, it settles, either by delivering the asset or, more often, by paying out the difference in cash. Traders who want to keep their position open must "roll" it into a new contract, which can add costs and complexity.

Perpetual futures, often shortened to "perps" or "perpetual swaps," work like futures, but with one key difference: they never expire. Traders can hold a position for as long as they like, provided they have enough collateral to keep it open.

The idea was proposed by Yale economist Robert Shiller in a 1993 paper as a way to create markets for assets that are hard to trade directly. Perps took off in crypto. Crypto exchange BitMEX, widely credited with bringing perps to crypto, launched a perpetual contract on Bitcoin in 2016. BitMEX itself closed in September 2026, but the product it pioneered has become the most traded instrument in crypto.

A perpetual contract never actually involves owning the asset. Traders deposit collateral, or "margin," and their profit or loss depends entirely on how the price moves.

When opening a perp position, a trader chooses a direction. Going long means betting the price will rise; going short means betting it will fall. Being able to short as easily as going long is one reason perps are popular: it lets traders profit from falling prices, or hedge assets they already own.

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets traders control a larger position than their collateral alone would allow. With 10x leverage, for example, $100 of margin controls a $1,000 position. If the asset's price rises 5%, the trader makes $50, a 50% return on their margin. But if it falls 5%, they lose $50, half of their margin.

Exchanges track two prices to manage this. The index price is the asset's price on spot markets, usually averaged across several exchanges. The mark price, based on the index and the perp market itself, is used to calculate profits and losses and to decide when positions must be closed. Using an averaged price makes it harder for a sudden spike on one exchange to trigger liquidations.

Because perps never expire, nothing forces their price to converge with the underlying asset, as traditional futures do at expiry. Instead, perps use a funding rate.

At regular intervals—most commonly every eight hours, though some platforms use shorter or longer schedules—traders on one side of the market pay traders on the other:

Decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, for example, pays funding every hour, at one-eighth of the rate calculated for an eight-hour period, to stay consistent with centralized exchanges.

Funding payments go directly between traders, not to the exchange. For traders holding positions over long periods, these payments can add up, and they can either boost or erode returns.

Funding rates also offer a window into market sentiment. Strongly positive funding suggests traders are crowded into bullish bets, while negative funding shows that bears are paying to stay short.

Rates for the same asset can also differ between exchanges. On November 19, 2026, Bitcoin's funding rate was 0.01% per eight hours on Hyperliquid, compared with 0.0037% on Binance, according to Hyperliquid's funding comparison tool.

Some traders aim to earn funding payments rather than bet on prices. In a "cash and carry" or basis trade, they buy an asset on the spot market and short the same amount through perps, collecting funding while their positions offset each other's price moves.

Leverage is a double-edged sword. Every perp position has a maintenance margin, the minimum collateral needed to keep it open. If losses push a trader's margin below that level, the exchange automatically closes the position, a process called liquidation. The trader typically loses most or all of the margin they put up.

The higher the leverage, the smaller the price move needed to trigger a liquidation. At 50x leverage, a 2% move against a position can wipe it out.

Liquidations can also feed on each other. When prices fall sharply, liquidated longs are forced to sell, pushing prices lower and triggering further liquidations, a so-called liquidation cascade. On October 10, 2025, the crypto market saw its largest liquidation event on record, with more than $19 billion in leveraged positions wiped out in a single day.

To cover losses when positions can't be closed in time, exchanges typically keep an insurance fund. In extreme cases, some use auto-deleveraging, closing profitable positions on the other side of the market to keep the system solvent.

Crypto perps trade on two main types of venue.

Centralized exchanges, such as Binance, Bybit and OKX, run their own order books and hold users' funds. They account for the vast majority of perps trading: Binance alone held about a third of centralized perps volume in early 2026, according to CoinGecko. Many are unavailable to U.S. customers.

Decentralized exchanges let traders use perps through smart contracts, while keeping control of their funds. Some, such as Hyperliquid and Solana-based Phoenix, run order books onchain, matching buyers and sellers much like a traditional exchange. Others, such as GMX and Jupiter, let traders trade against a shared pool of liquidity provided by other users.

Decentralized perps are growing fast. Their trading volume jumped from $1.5 trillion in 2024 to $6.38 trillion in 2025, led by Hyperliquid, CoinGecko found. Decentralized exchanges' share of perps trading volume peaked at 13% in late 2025 before slipping to 10% in April 2026, while their share of open interest, the total value of open positions, reached 13.5%.

In the U.S., perps developed largely on offshore exchanges, outside the reach of the CFTC, but that has started to change. In July 2025, Coinbase introduced CFTC-regulated "perpetual-style" futures for U.S. customers, with five-year expiries and up to 10x leverage. Two months later, CFTC-regulated exchange Bitnomial launched what it called the first U.S. perpetual futures, with 25-year terms and eight-hour funding. In May 2026, the CFTC approved a Bitcoin perpetual futures contract from prediction market Kalshi and set out a framework for listing more, and in June, Kraken rolled out perps to eligible U.S. customers through Bitnomial, which its parent company had acquired.

Some of these contracts, including Kalshi's and Bitnomial's original design, have long but fixed terms of 25 years, so are not strictly perpetual. The regulator says perps can be offered on CFTC-registered exchanges under federal oversight, and has advised U.S. traders to avoid offshore venues.

The shift has been contested. CME Group, the largest U.S. futures exchange, has sued the CFTC, arguing that perps are swaps rather than futures under U.S. law. The CFTC has nonetheless continued to approve more perps platforms, starting with Backpack.

Perps are now spreading beyond crypto. Exchanges have begun listing perps tracking stocks, stock indexes, commodities such as gold and oil, and currencies, giving traders around-the-clock exposure to markets that normally close in the evening and on weekends.

Those products carry the same mechanics, and the same risks, as crypto perps. They track an asset's price without giving traders ownership of the asset itself, so a perp on a company's stock doesn't come with voting rights or dividends.

Perps can be useful tools for hedging and speculation, but they are among the riskiest products in crypto:

Decrypt users can now trade perps directly alongside the news, through the Decrypt Money Account. The service is powered by Phoenix, a decentralized exchange on Solana, and lets users go long or short on crypto, stocks and commodities.

Availability varies by region, and as with any leveraged product, traders should only risk money they can afford to lose.

Decrypt offers perpetual futures trading through the Decrypt Money Account, powered by Phoenix.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region. Nothing in this guide is investment advice.

Ben Zartman Splices Climbing Slings In His Garage. They’re the Best I’ve Ever Used.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ben Zartman at work splicing slings in his garage. (Photo: Courtesy Ben Zartman)

When Ben Zartman learned to climb near Mexico City in the early 1990s, no one he knew could afford climbing gear.

“All of us were poor, and all the gear was imported and so expensive,” he says. “Most of the guys at the crag were sewing their own shoes or making their own cams.” Backpacks, harnesses, quickdraws—if they could sew it themselves, they did. At the time, Zartman was in high school. He didn’t have a job, but he did have a knack for making stuff. It never occurred to him that doing so might be sketchy until he arrived in Yosemite for the first time in 1995 and partners refused to climb on his rack. They were astonished he’d made his own gear. He was astonished that they’d bought theirs.

Later that year, when a government shutdown forced Zartman out of Joshua Tree, he hitchhiked to San Diego and bummed a ride to Baja with some sailors. Over the next few years, he split his time between big walls and boats, getting better at both kinds of rigging all the while. Eventually he got married, had three daughters, and spent five years sailing around the world on a boat he’d built himself. Eventually, he got work in a rigging shop in Rhode Island. He spliced custom slings for workaday sailors, then for open-ocean racing boats.

“At one point, I was sitting in the shop, splicing these high-end Dyneema loops, and I thought, man, if I had these in Yosemite, I would have been the coolest kid at the lodge cafeteria,” he says. And he likely would have: the spliced, covered loops used in sailing are seamless: no bar tacks or stitching. Instead, one end of the loop is woven into the other like a snake eating its tail. When you pull, the ends suction against each other (Zartman compares it to a finger-trap toy), making the loop stronger under load than bartacked Dyneema.

“The idea fomented for a while in my mind, and in 2021 I started reaching out to every climbing gear manufacturer I could think of,” Zartman says. Letters and samples went out to Black Diamond, Metolius, Sterling, and others, asking them if they’d be interested in partnering on a product. Hardly anyone responded.

So Zartman did what he’d always done: he took the DIY approach. In 2023, he launched his company, Zartman Rigging. He joined the UIAA, mailed in slings for lab testing, and registered for UIAA labels. Once his gear was certified, the renowned gear shop IME, in North Conway, New Hampshire, started selling it. Rock and Snow in New Paltz, New York, and IME in Salt Lake City, Utah, were next. As his following grew, so did his offerings: a featherlight personal anchor system, seamless rappel back-ups, a rope-carabiner alternative called the Dreamlink, and a tiny rappel plate designed for 6-mm cord. The ideas come from Zartman’s experience climbing Yosemite walls; the materials come from his time on boats.

“The research and development budget in the [sailboat] racing scene is so big,” he says. “It’s a great place to take technology from. I’m at that edge—I make ropes for the fanciest racing boats in the world, so I’m able to bring that into the climbing world pretty quickly.”

The results are wild. The Dreamlink has a breaking strength of 43 kN (or, rather, he tested it up to 42 kN in a UIAA lab, and that’s when it broke their machine). The seamless runners are tagged 24 kN but break at 27kN. A fancy UV coating makes them essentially sun-proof. They don’t expire like other soft goods; you only have to retire them if the core becomes exposed. I have a double-length seamless runner that I use for building anchors and extending rappels. No bartack means easy setup, it racks well, and it’s pretty light. (Most traditionally manufactured Dyneema slings are tagged at 22kN. Mammut’s 120-centimeter Contact Sling weighs 28 grams, while Zartman’s version is 32 grams, the same as Black Diamond’s Infinity Cord.)

Zartman has a strong social media fan club, but he has plenty of critics too. Mountain Project commenters, he said, can be particularly vicious. Many scoff at the idea of a soft shackle or a seamless runner as gimmicky or unsafe. He’s not too bothered.

“Ray Jardine got scolded by all the Valley locals when he showed up with Friends,” he says, citing the first-ever cams. When other companies started making camming devices in the ‘80s, Jardine’s critics were forced to bite their tongues. Zartman is seeing a similar arc with his own gear. Several other climbing brands have started marketing soft shackles similar to Zartman’s Dreamlink. And in 2025, Black Diamond released its Infinity Cord, a Dyneema sling that uses the same bartack-free construction as Zartman’s and the same covered-loop splicing technique that sailors have used for decades. Black Diamond called it a “breakthrough in the evolution of cordelette engineering.”

Zartman says he’s unaffected by the new competition. “If there are people copying the concept, that’s good because that means the concept is good,” he says. In the meantime, he’s keeping his head down, focused on fulfilling orders and prototyping his next batch of inventions.

Zartman Rigging is gaining recognition, albeit slowly. Zartman does no paid advertising; about 80 percent of his business comes from Instagram. His top-shelf pricing likely tempers growth as well. For context, a 120-centimeter Zartman Rigging sling is $63, versus $40 for Black Diamond’s Infinity Cord or $14 for a basic Dyneema Trango runner of the same length.

“Because I make everything out of the nicest materials and by hand, it’s the most expensive gear on the market. It’s a luxury brand,” he says. “Which is ironic given that I started in Mexico with my own homemade climbing shoes.”

Still, he produces enough to keep busy. Recently, he was able to hire one of his daughters part-time to help him splice, tag, and quality-test each product. He’s now a member of the UIAA safety commission, helping the organization design new testing protocols around skinny ropes and other novel forms of cord. He’s shipping to Alaska and Hawaii. People have started recognizing him in gear shops and at crags. Things are taking off—despite what Mountain Projectors may say.

“To a lot of people’s surprise,” he laughs, “I’ve sold a lot of gear.”

You won't find this fan favorite feature in GTA 6

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Replaying missions is possible, but not easy.

Some of the more hardcore fans, running on little-to-no sleep, might be working their way through the missions in the Grand Theft Auto 6. Unlike in its predecessor though, GTA 6 has no option to replay completed story missions. That marks a major change from the previous game, which allowed players to run back finished mission from the pause menu, mostly to retry its skill challenges. (Those haven't reappeared, either.)

Even after more than 100 hours playing the game for his review, GamesRadar editor-in-chief Josh West said that he found no replay option. Other early-access reviewers are reporting the same. So, the only way back into a mission, at least for now, is to load a manual save made before it started, and before autosave overwrites itself.

Mission replay was first added to The Ballad of Gay Tony, the second Grand Theft Auto 4 expansion, back in 2009. It also introduced a scoring system that graded players on secondary mission goals like completion time, damage taken and headshots. Replay was introduced essentially so players could go back and get a better score. Then, with GTA 5, those goals became bronze, silver and gold medals, and achievements tied to attaining them. Rockstar's Red Dead Redemption 2's also had a "Gold Rush" trophy, which also asked for 70 gold medals out of the 104 available via its story missions.

GTA 6 has no gold medal objectives at all, at least as things stand now. Instead, challenges can be found in their own section of the progress menu. They cover broader goals rather than being mission-specific, and mostly unlock cheats once the story is completed. Rockstar North's co-studio head Rob Nelson told IGN that Rockstar had made "a very conscious effort to open things up where possible" in GTA 6's missions. Players will now find that some missions suggest an optimal route that players can ignore, for example, with the job turning into a police chase rather than ending in failure.

Red Dead Redemption 2 players long complained that straying from set paths could fail missions. That, along with GTA 5's mission medals enforcing a linear playstyle, seems to be the reason why Rockstar left them out.

GTA 6 gives us 15 save slots. That might seem generous on the face of it, but it's not all that many for a campaign that has more than 90 main missions. Sharper minds than I have worked out that keeping a route back to everything would mean a fresh manual save roughly every 6.6 percent of the way through the game.

The missing mission repeat feature probably comes down to how sprawling GTA 6's story is. Entire missions can play out differently based on decisions players made earlier, sometimes without knowing they were making them. A heist, for example, can be buttery smooth or turn into an awful mess depending on gameplay completed hours earlier. Story choices also shape the wider world, down to which characters live and die. Reloading a mission and playing it in isolation would ignore the choices that set it up originally.

It's not all bad news: Races, stunts, pool and other minigames are repeatable, as are shop robberies and the bank jobs as the story unlocks. Robbed locations gray out on the map and reset after a cooldown.

And obviously GTA 6's story was designed to support repeat playthroughs, but that's a big commitment. Nelson himself — the game's lead dev — has said his playthrough took 80 hours, covering the main story and some optional goals. In GTA 5, you could jump back into a favorite mission in a few minutes, but now, playing one differently means planning saves in advance or committing to another full run. Whether that changes remains to be seen.

What Every Home Needs for an Extended Power Outage

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Americans are “pantry-loading” following a cascade of technology outages that have disrupted email, grounded flights, and interrupted banking services—while feuling fears that the failures could be signs of something bigger.

Costco and Walmart have reported increased purchases of emergency supplies, from food and water to batteries and generators. As of Nov. 15, 21 of the 26 high-priced generators offered on Costco's website were sold out, according to Bloomberg.

"Consumer anxiety following the Microsoft Outlook outage, the ground stoppage for all U.S. airline flights and apocalyptic concerns flaring up across social media have all contributed to increased emergency prep sales," David Bellinger, an analyst for Mizuho, a Japanese investment bank, wrote in a recent research note.

Federal officials say they've found no evidence that the outages are connected or resulted from coordinated cyberattacks. But while the fears driving the rush to stockpile may be unfounded, the threat of prolonged disruptions is all-too real.

In August, severe storms knocked out power to nearly 375,000 customers across Northwest Indiana, leaving some without electricity for nearly two weeks.

A historic ice storm in January caused more than 330,000 outages across Mississippi and Louisiana, where some residents faced similarly long waits.

Both emergencies stretched far beyond the familiar three-day supply of food and water—and exposed how quickly households can lose access to basic necessities.

“A three-day emergency kit is still a good starting point, but I’d consider it the minimum rather than the actual goal,” says Brian Armentrout of Chariot Energy. “In Texas, we’ve seen severe weather events where power outages and other disruptions can last well beyond 72 hours.”

But preparing for the next emergency means more than filling a pantry. It means knowing how long your home can function without the systems that keep it safe—and having a plan for when it can't.

The familiar three-day kit is still a vital part of emergency preparedness, but the American Red Cross recommends that amount for an evacuation kit.

For households sheltering at home, the recommendation is much longer: two weeks of food and water.

Daniel Kilburn, an emergency-planning consultant and retired U.S. Army senior infantry drill sergeant, makes a similar distinction.

“The three-day emergency kit is still good if you're actually evacuating. It should not take more than three days to get to where you need to go,” he says. “However, based on current power outages happening around the country, eight to 10 days is not unreasonable."

But most households aren't prepared for that: 69% of respondents said they had assembled emergency supplies, according to the Federal Emergency Management Agency's latest National Household Survey. Among that group, just 34% said their supplies could last more than two weeks.

Kilburn recommends having enough food and water for every member of the household to last a full two weeks. But that doesn't mean buying two weeks of emergency supplies all at once.

Instead, he recommends gradually adding extra shelf-stable foods and water during normal shopping trips, then rotating those foods through the kitchen so the emergency supply functions more like a well-stocked pantry.

“Water is one of the biggest things people tend to overlook,” Armentrout says. “During an extended outage, water service can also be disrupted, so it’s important to have enough bottled water on hand for drinking and basic needs.”

Winter Storm Uri showed how those failures can overlap. The 2021 storm caused power losses for 69% of Texans and water service disruptions for 49%, according to the Texas State Auditor's Office.

The Red Cross recommends one gallon of water per person per day. For a family of four, that works out to 12 gallons for three days—or 56 gallons for two weeks.

And while food may be more intuitive to store than water, it presents a different problem: how to cook it without power.

A closed refrigerator generally keeps food cold for only about four hours without power, according to FoodSafety.gov. A full freezer can maintain its temperature for about 48 hours; a half-full freezer, about 24 hours.

That makes shelf-stable foods a good choice. But Kilburn warns that households should also have a safe way to boil water or prepare food if the power stays out.

A cellphone may become one of the most useful tools in a blackout, and useless within hours without a way to recharge it.

“Backup power is another area people often underestimate,” Armentrout says. “A portable battery bank, generator, or small solar charger can help keep phones and other essential devices operating when the power is out for several days.”

The Red Cross also recommends a battery-powered or hand-crank radio, backup batteries and chargers, and a paper copy of important phone numbers.

But phones are only one electrical dependency inside a modern home. Kilburn recommends mentally walking through an ordinary day and asking what stops working without electricity.

“Let's say you decide it's time to evacuate and your car is in the garage, how are you going to get the garage door open?” he says.

Medical equipment is also a vital consideration. More than 3 million Medicare beneficiaries rely on electricity-dependent durable medical and assistive equipment, according to the Department of Health and Human Services.

For these households, the Red Cross recommends establishing a backup plan in advance.

Generators can provide backup power, but they carry their own risks: carbon-monoxide poisoning from misuse. Nearly 100 Americans die each year in poisonings caused by portable generators, according to the U.S. Consumer Product Safety Commission.

To stay safe, never operate a generator in an enclosed space like your home or garage—even with doors or windows open. Keep them outdoors and more than 20 feet from windows, doors, and vents, and make sure you also have a battery-powered or battery-backup carbon-monoxide detector.

Eventually, the biggest question during a prolonged outage may not be how many supplies remain, but whether the home itself is still safe.

“Before an outage, close blinds or blackout curtains to reduce the amount of heat coming into the house,” Armentrout says. “If the power goes out, stay in the coolest part of the home, which is typically a room on the lower floor away from direct sunlight.”

Battery-powered fans can help, but only to a point. The Centers for Disease Control says fans should be used indoors only when temperatures are below 90 degrees Fahrenheit. Above that, using a fan can actually increase body temperature.

That means one of the most important parts of a blackout plan can't be stored in a closet: somewhere else to go.

Kilburn recommends finding out in advance where cooling centers would operate during a major outage. And Armentrout says households should establish their threshold for leaving before conditions deteriorate.

“It becomes unsafe when your home can no longer meet your basic needs,” he says. “That could be caused by a variety of things, from temperatures becoming too hot or too cold, to running low on water, necessary medications, or having the ability to run critical medical equipment.”

So preparing for two weeks without electricity doesn't mean planning to stay inside a powerless home for two weeks no matter what.

It means having enough food, water, and backup power to preserve your options—and a plan for where to go when staying home is no longer one of them.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

Real Estate Notes Make You the Lender – Check the Collateral Before You Lend

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

A real estate note lets you lend against someone else’s property and collect the interest. Before you put money in, confirm your loan is no more than 70% of the property’s appraised value and gets repaid first if the property sells. If you can’t check that yourself, spread small amounts across many loans.

The interest is the appeal. The property is your protection, but only if you can claim it and sell it for at least what you’re owed.

You can buy a performing note, one whose borrower is paying, or pay less than the loan’s balance for a non-performing note, one whose borrower has stopped paying. You can lend straight to a house flipper. Or you can buy slices of many loans through a real estate crowdfunding platform. The real estate bonds some apps sell are loans to the company itself and give you no claim on a particular property.

A default can end in foreclosure, and foreclosures are slow. Homes foreclosed in the second quarter of 2026 had spent an average of 563 days in the process, according to property data firm ATTOM. Texas averaged 155 days and New York 2,007. You can’t cash out early, either. You get your money back as the borrower repays, or by finding someone to buy the note.

Before you buy a note or lend directly, check five things:

Say you lend $100,000 at 10% to a flipper on a house worth $160,000. You collect $10,000 a year until the project stalls and the payments stop. Then you pay a lawyer and keep up the property’s taxes and insurance until the foreclosure ends and the house sells. The $60,000 between the loan and the value has to cover those costs and any drop in price.

Unless you can run all five checks on every loan, spread the same $100,000 across 50 loans of $2,000 each, so one default puts a fiftieth of your money at risk instead of all of it.

Find a platform that sells slices of many loans among the best real estate crowdfunding sites.

Toyota’s Corolla Cross Deck Is a Forbidden Fruit Rival To the Ford Maverick

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The four-door unibody pickup, unfortunately, seems only destined for Latin America and the Caribbean for the moment.

Toyota has had incredible success with pickup trucks in the United States of America. The Tacoma has become one of America’s perennial best-selling vehicles, while the Tundra has carved out a sustainable niche in America’s grueling full-size segment. But there’s arguably room for more.

Rumors have long suggested Toyota is working on a compact, Ford Maverick-fighting pickup that would fill the truck lineup’s glaring need for a cheaper and more efficient option. Dealers have reportedly been asking Toyota for a compact pickup for several years. Mark Templin, Toyota’s COO in the United States, confirmed Toyota is “looking at” bringing a smaller pickup to the U.S. back in May 2025. And now, Toyota has just unveiled a new pickup that could fit that bill … for Latin American and Caribbean markets.

Toyota revealed a new compact pickup called the Corolla Cross Deck on Wednesday during its inauguration of a new production facility in Sococaba, Brazil, and it will enter production in the first half of 2027. According to Motor1 Brazil, the Corolla Cross Deck shares a TNGA-C platform with the Corolla sedan and Corolla Cross SUV. Toyota did not provide full specs, but did reveal a hybrid version—likely featuring the 1.8-liter 122-horsepower powertrain Toyota uses for the Corolla Cross in that market.

Sadly, Toyota has not confirmed potential availability for the Corolla Cross Deck in the American market. The automaker did not respond to a Road & Track inquiry about its potential availability by the time of publication, but we’ll update this story if we receive a response.

On paper, a Corolla-based compact pickup would likely be the easiest route for Toyota to bring a small, cheap pickup to the United States in the foreseeable future. Toyota already builds the Corolla and Corolla Cross at its Mazda Toyota Manufacturing Facility in Alabama; a new truck truck could be built on the same line—and even spawn potential rebadged variants for Toyota partners Mazda and Subaru.

In the American market, the Toyota Corolla Cross uses 2.0-liter gas and hybrid engines. That powertrain could give Toyota options for a 169-horsepower base gas model truck—potentially starting below $30,000—and a 196-hp hybrid version that could eclipse 40 miles per gallon.

That said, one could also see Toyota needing a different small truck approach to resonate with an American audience. One of Ford’s great innovations with the Maverick was that it looks more like the F-150 and Ranger than the Escape crossover it shared a platform with. And even if a new Toyota pickup were to share its bones with the Corolla, Toyota may want to brand it as an extension of the pickup line, rather than the Corolla family.

‘Vanderpump Rules’ Alum Ariana Madix Snaps Up a $2.9 Million West Village Loft With Boyfriend Daniel Wai

“Vanderpump Rules” alum Ariana Madix and boyfriend Daniel Wai have taken an exciting new step together after snapping up a $2.9 million loft in New York City.

The couple—who have been in a long-distance relationship since 2023—purchased a recently-renovated condominium in the Printing House building in the West Village, according to Curbed.

Although Madix still holds ties to Los Angeles—with a $1.6 million home she purchased in 2024 and her sandwich shop, Something About Her—she’s been spending much more time in the Big Apple in recent years. It’s where Wai, a personal trainer, lives full time.

Of course, there’s also her Broadway career. The “Love Island USA” host made her on-stage debut as Roxie Hart in “Chicago” in January 2024 and is currently starring as Connie Francis in the musical, “Just in Time” at the Circle in the Square Theatre. Her run has been extended through Nov. 28.

The 1,763-square-foot residence boasts 15-foot ceilings and spans two levels. According to the listing, the home has been configured into a three-bedroom, 2.5-bathroom layout, and includes “top-of-the-line finishes” throughout.

The entrance of the unit opens into the “modern” kitchen, which has a dedicated dining space and leads way to the double-height living room. This floor of the home also has a powder room and a “massive primary bedroom” with a “luxurious five-piece bathroom and double walk-in closets.”

A mezzanine level—accessible by a statement-making steel staircase—hosts the second and third bedrooms, as well as a white marble-finished second full bathroom.

“The space is made complete by abundant storage options throughout, intelligent home pre-wiring, remotely operable central HVAC, and automatic blinds,” the listing adds.

Aside from its prime location in the West Village, the building itself offers quite a few amenities.

“The Printing House is an iconic, fully-staffed, industrial building that anchors the West Village and was reimagined into luxury loft style residences,” the listing explains.

The security was likely a draw for Madix, whose California home was broken into just last month. Burglars shattered the home’s back door, stealing jewelry and other valuable possessions on Oct. 17.

The former printing factory was first built in 1910, and was later converted into residential condos in the 1980s. Today, there’s an Equinox on-site, a rooftop pool, and a beautifully-landscaped sun deck that offers breathtaking views of the Manhattan skyline.

While fans may associate the reality TV star with the West Coast, New York City is actually where she got her start.

Before she placed roots in L.A., the Melbourne, FL, native moved to New York after college in hopes of pursuing a career in sketch comedy. She became a bartender at Butter, a famed Midtown Manhattan restaurant located within the Cassa Hotel.

“Some parts of it are like riding a bike,” Madix told Extra in 2023, when she had returned to New York for the first time. “Taking the train and walking everywhere, things like that. I was like, ‘Ok, I remember how to do this.’”

The actress noted that in some ways, it feels like “a different city” than the one she had lived in as a recent college graduate.

“I was here pre-Instagram, pre-iPhone, pre-social media. It is very different in a lot of ways, but I think that’s just, you know, the world at large,” she said.

The new pad isn’t the only real estate move Madix has made this year.

Following a yearslong legal battle with her ex-boyfriend, Tom Sandoval, the former couple finally cut ties with their Valley Village abode in an off-market transaction this spring, pulling in $3.1 million.

In April 2026, TMZ reported that Madix and Sandoval’s listing agent had been preparing to put the five-bedroom home on the market, but an anonymous buyer swooped in to make an offer the former couple simply couldn’t refuse.

"We’ve been keeping a secret! We had this beautiful home in Valley Village that we were prepping for market and before we could post about it or list it, we got it under contract for full-price off-market!" listing agent Ashley Fultz with DMF Realty wrote in an Instagram caption.

The pair purchased the six-bedroom modern farmhouse-style dwelling in 2019 for $2.08 million. Before their highly publicized split in 2023, they completely transformed the home with a massive renovation which included a live greenery wall and a dedicated room for content creation and social media.

Your Favorite THC Drinks Are About to Be Illegal

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Tuesday, more than two dozen veterans organizations sent an open letter to bipartisan congressional leaders demanding something that a few years ago might have been unheard of: better access to legal weed. “Veterans have sacrificed for our country, and they deserve safe, responsible options for managing chronic pain, PTSD, anxiety, sleep problems, and other health conditions,” it read. “Many veterans… rely on hemp products as an alternative to opioids. If Congress fails to act, the products they depend on will suddenly become illegal.”

That’s because on Dec. 11, a new law that limits the amount of THC allowed in hemp will go into effect, eradicating the cannabis market as Americans have come to know it. The colorful cans of THC sodas on Trader Joe’s shelves will be gone, as will the sleep-aid gummies from Target and and the bottles of THC “liquor” at Total Wines. “Anyone who produces the seed, grows a crop, processes the crop, makes a product, sells it to the shelf, and the people who buy it — all these people will be impacted,” explains Greg Serrao, co-founder of Willie’s Remedy, one of the country’s best-selling cannabis drinks.

The new definition will close an eight-year loophole that allowed the CBD market to take off, followed by the federally legal THC market. Veterans, farmers, and cannabis entrepreneurs agree that more regulations are needed to help finally wrangle what was, for a long time, a frontier industry. “A lot of people paint this picture of hemp as this wild west,” says Serrao. “That’s not true.”

What is true is that unless Congress acts to amend the new rules, in less than a month, a $28 billion industry will disappear. “Something like 65 percent of our customers are senior citizens, and 70 percent are over 45,” says Willie’s Remedy’s other co-founder, Forrest Dein.

Sens. Tim Sheehy (R-Mont.) and Amy Klobuchar (D-Minn.) introduced a bill to the Senate on Tuesday that would halt the new rules, instead offering a new set of regulations around hemp-derived THC products, including age limits, testing and packaging requirements, and other guardrails around the plant. “Our bill will put every bad actor in the hemp space out of business and keep intoxicating products out of the hands of children,” Sheehy, a veteran, said in a press release. “It will also stop Congress from turning the farmers, veterans, and cancer patients who rely on these products into criminals overnight.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

But unless that advances soon, the businesses who rely on hemp — including the struggling farmers who were told hemp would be the crop to save them — will be shit out of luck.  “The collateral damage is that we axe this American commodity that has so much hope,” explains Morgan Tweet, who runs IND Hemp, a company that processes hemp into products like rope and fiber. “It’s really something special for American farmers, and for American businesses, and manufacturers. There’s nothing in our generation that comes close to being able to show the potential that hemp has with how diverse and how useful of a crop it is, and the magnitude of products you can make from it.”

It all started back in 2018, when Sen. Mitch McConnell accidentally legalized weed — kind of.

On Dec. 20, 2018, President Donald Trump signed the Agricultural Improvement Act, colloquially known as the Farm Bill, into law. In between protections for dairy farmers and changes to the federal food assistance program was a monumental change: for the first time since 1937, growing hemp would be legal in the United States. McConnell himself championed the change, positioning it as a boon to farmers in his home state of Kentucky. The text of the bill defined hemp as a cannabis plant that contained no more than .3 percent delta-nine THC, weed’s intoxicating compound, on a dry-weight basis. “I do not have any plans to endorse the legalization of marijuana,” McConnell said, noting that hemp and marijuana were “two separate plants.” Related Content Summer Walker Files $20 Million Cross-Complaint Against Interscope Julio Iglesias Under Investigation for Sexual Abuse Claims What I Learned About Genocide From the Rohingya Muslims Mullin Says Skipping LSU Game Amid FAA Crisis Would Have Caused ‘Confusion’

By then, hemp was already a popular crop in Europe, where it had long been used for everything from oil to rope. It was also at the center of the growing CBD industry. CBD, another cannabinoid found in hemp, was not intoxicating, like THC, but was starting to be used medicinally for things like epilepsy and pain management.

Once hemp was legalized, American farmers started planting the crop. Some varieties focused on bushy leaves and heavy stalks, great for industrial uses. But others had big flowers, perfect for extracting CBD. The CBD boom was expected; what wasn’t was that scientists quickly figured out a process to convert CBD into THC — and the hemp-derived THC market took off. Not only did they figure out how to make delta-9 THC, the cannabinoid that is known to get us high, they also started to make a wide variety of other kinds of cannabinoids, including delta-8 THC (a slightly less potent intoxicant that is present in trace amounts in the plant), as well as some that don’t actually occur naturally. It was all legal because the products were derived from hemp, not marijuana, which is a Schedule I controlled substance.

Many of these companies self-policed, testing the products themselves and going through established liquor frameworks in order to be sold at national chains. Others, however, skirted the rules, offering shady products made with questionable cannabinoids and no real testing or oversight. This so-called “gas station weed” started to make folks nervous — particularly McConnell. So when Congress was putting together a bill to reopen the government last fall, after a record-long shutdown, McConnell added an amendment to the 2018 definition of hemp to get rid of the market as we know it.

The new law will change the definition of legal hemp. Previously, hemp was allowed to have up to .3 percent THC as measured in its dry weight. Hemp farmers could grow crops that adhered to this, then processors could extract cannabinoids, and companies put them into products so that they had enough potency to see effects. The new law, however, looks at the final product, restricting anything with more than .4 milligrams of THC per container.

There are two parts to the law, explains Neil M. Willner, an attorney with Vicente LLP, a New York-based firm specializing in cannabis law. The first went into effect on Nov. 12, and banned the sale of all synthetic THC products, or THC compounds that are not found naturally in the cannabis plant. On Dec. 11, the low-dose sodas, liquors, and gummies that have become so popular — and usually contain five or 10 mg of THC per serving — will no longer be legal on a federal level. People in areas with state-legal cannabis programs can still go to a dispensary to buy THC products, but the companies built around hemp-derived cannabinoids — and the people in states without legal weed — will be out of luck.

It will also make it nearly impossible to grow hemp, too. “We’ve talked to a lot of hemp farmers, and it’s very difficult for them to manage the THC content of their crops,” says Dein. “It’ll push a lot of farmers out of business.” Tweet, owner of IND Hemp, the processing facility, adds that the new law doesn’t define what a “container” actually is. “I ship 55-gallon drums of hemp seed oil all over the country, and by definition, that’s a container, right?” she says. “Like, we do make a product that likely will go above that 0.4 milligram [limit] just because of that volume. Even though hemp seed oil doesn’t have a lot of cannabinoids in it, it does have some, and so there is some risk to our business.”

In one word, bad. Take, for example, Texas, where outgoing Lieutenant Governor Dan Patrick introduced a bill over the summer that would have banned all hemp products that contained THC. This was wildly unpopular with many of the 1.5 million veterans who live in the state, among others. Outgoing Governor Greg Abbott ended up vetoing the bill, instead establishing guidelines for age and marketing restrictions. In November, Patrick lost his bid for reelection, as did Abbott and other Republicans statewide. “I think that’s one of the reasons Texas went blue,” says Serrao. Trending Stories John Mayer Transports Michael J. Fox Back to the Future With Chuck Berry Performance CMA Awards 2026: The Complete Winners List Halestorm, Brothers Osborne, Hardy Sing for Brooks & Dunn at Ferocious 2026 CMA Tribute Stephen Wilson Jr. and Chris Stapleton Collab on ‘I’m a Song’ at 2026 CMA Awards

Tim Jensen, CEO of the consulting firm War Party Inc., which advises companies on how best to connect with veterans, as well as a signatory to the open letter sent to Congress, points out that it’s more than bad optics — there’s a very real health risk to veterans if the rug is pulled out from under the industry. “We have a large community of individuals of 55 and older that are experiencing a lot of inflammation and pain-related problems that are wanting to find alternative modalities that are not opioid related, and that is big,” he says. “You’re finding the veteran community really leaning into these opportunities to get away from alcohol.”

Everyone who spoke to Rolling Stone for this article agreed that further federal regulations are needed in order for the industry to safely grow. Getting rid of full synthetics, as the law did on Nov. 12, is a positive step, they say, but “what we don’t have is purity standards,” explains Tweet. “We don’t have any regulations, so it’s only the people that self-regulate, that put good products on the market that are the ones you should be trusting. But the reality is that consumers have no idea what’s in their product. They don’t know what they’re buying.”

Imax Inks Largest Cinema Deal In U.K. For New Locations

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The agreement for U.K. and European theater locations with Moshe “Mooky” Greidinger's Showcase Cinemas gets Imax back in business with the former Cineworld Group CEO.

Imax is back in business with former Cineworld Group CEO Moshe “Mooky” Greidinger after signing a deal for 13 new laser projection systems at 12 theaters in the Showcase Cinemas circuit in the U.K. and Europe.

Terms of the agreement were not disclosed, but Imax has signed its first deal with Showcase Cinemas, and also with a longtime business partner. “I’ve been in the Imax business for decades and the value of its technology, brand, and strategic partnership to exhibitors have grown exponentially in that time,” Greidinger said in a statement after his recent acquisition of the 16-venue Showcase Cinemas circuit from Skydance.

“There is no doubt that Imax plays a significant role in the results of the industry and I am sure it will continue to grow and attract new audience and become even more significant in the coming years. I look forward to working with Rich and his team and making the Imax Experience a cornerstone of the new Showcase Cinemas in the U.K.,” he added.

The new laser projection systems will go in 12 U.K. locations and another in Europe. Regal owner Cineworld in 2023 emerged from Chapter 11 bankruptcy proceeding in the U.S. via a restructuring, with Greidinger and his management team eventually leaving the British cinema operator.

“Mooky is a visionary leader and an excellent partner who recognized the value in Imax long before our recent run of record-breaking success. We’re excited to be back in business with his team at Showcase Cinemas UK,” Richard Gelfond, CEO of Imax, said in his own statement.

Five of the new Imax locations will open across the U.K. in 2027, with the remaining locations to open in the next few years, Imax said. The deal also comes as Imax shows momentum in new theater signings after the release of Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey on its screens, and ahead of the debut of Dune: Part Three at Imax locations worldwide.

The deal announcement with Showcase Cinemas also comes a day after Imax said it had promoted Giovanni Dolci to the newly created role of chief operating officer, overseeing the company’s expanding global theater network.
